ACROMASTIGUM_AUSTRALIA_2014  (1) 
often bulging and coalescing, margin of leaf thick walled; oil-bodies not observed; cuticle smooth. Underleaves
imbricate, insertion transverse but sometimes the lateral margins slightly decurrent, somewhat spreading to
almost squarrose, large (c. 3/4 size of lateral leaves), 1.2–1.7 × as wide as stem, suborbicular, 270–545 μm
long, 335–465 μm wide, not auriculate at base; apex truncate, subentire to emarginate, sinuses vestigal, lobes
rounded. Asexual reproduction not observed. Androecial branches slightly stalked (c. 100 μm), sometimes
developed in abundance; bracts very slightly bifid (<1/20) or entire, deeply saccate, rounded to bluntly acute,
1-androus, c. 440–490 μm long, 400–545 μm wide; bracteoles slightly emarginate (with papilla), ovate,
295–360 μm long, 280–360 μm wide. Gynoecia produced on short branches. Bracts and bracteoles similar, in
several series, innermost largest, larger ones usually bisbifid, central sinus acute (30–40°) and split 1/3–1/2 length
of bract, secondary lobes much smaller (<1/10 length of bract), ovate, margins with scattered 1- or 2-celled
laciniae and papillae; cells rectangular to almost isodiametric, length:width ratio 1.2–4.8, corners rounded
by small trigones; oil-bodies in scattered patches towards apex, 1–3 per cell, ± botryoidal, 2–5 μm diam.,
clear. Perianth c. 4 mm long when fully developed, slenderly ovoid-cylindrical, narrowed at mouth, 1-stratose
throughout, cells rectangular to elongate-rectangular, length:width ratio 1.2–5.5, thick-walled, trigones present
in mid and basal regions, tangential walls thickened in upper regions and up to 10 μm thick; oil-bodies present,
particularly in mid-region of perianth, 1–4 per cell, spherical, 4–5 μm diam., clear. Sporophyte; seta with
12–14 rows of large, hyaline epidermal cells filled with numerous tiny oil-bodies (which may give the cells a
dark appearance in transverse section) surrounding 15–18 rows of smaller interior cells; capsule inner layer
with nodular thickenings and occasionally semiannular bands; epidermal layer similar. Spores 13–16 μm diam.
Variation: Individuals of Acromastigum cavifolium exhibit some variation in shoot stature and the size of
associated leaves and underleaves. The only inter-individual variation observed during this study was in plant
colour, which varies from golden-brown to green; apparently in association with sun exposure.
Distribution and ecology: A. cavifolium is found in Australia (Tasmania) and New Zealand (North and South
Islands) and, like most Acromastigum species, is widespread but infrequently collected. Jarman describes
Tasmanian A. cavifolium as an understorey epiphyte (Jarman and Fuhrer 1995) but the species has also been
collected from damp soil and humus in seepage zones receiving a moderate amount of light (NSW285475);
a microsite more typical of A. verticale.
In New Zealand A. cavifolium is almost always encountered as an epiphyte in cool hyperhumid forests where
it inhabits bark of a wide range of species, including Agathis australis, Manoao colensoi, Olearia colensoii and
beech species including Nothofagus cliffortioides and N. menziesii. It can be found growing with an equally
diverse array of bryophytes including Brevianthus flavus, Lepicolea attenuata, Radula multiamentula, Acrochila
biserialis, Acroscyphella phoenicorhiza, and species of Chiloscyphus, Trichocolea, Telaranea, Lepidozia, Bazzania,
as well as with the fern Hymenophyllum armstrongii.
Recognition: The isophyllous shoots with nearly entire, triangular, concave leaves and underleaves in
combination with the production of ventral flagelliform branches are distinctive within the Australasian
bryophyte flora. Acromastigum cavifolium could only be confused with A. verticale, but the two species differ
in microhabitat, shoot orientation, and leaf and underleaf shape. Generally Acromastigum cavifolium is an
epiphyte or lithophyte whose isophyllous shoots grow horizontally along the substrate. Acromastigum verticale

4.5.3 Key Taxa
The bryophyte species which had a frequency of occurrence of 25% or more on either
phorophyte are arbitrarily considered to be key epiphyte species. For the mosses they
included; Dicranoloma      mem.iesii,    Hypnum     chrysogaster.     Wijkia    extenuata,
LeplOrileca gaudichaudii. Rhizogonium novae-hollandiae and Dicranoloma robuslLlm
var. selosum. For the bepatics (hey included; Ba72llnia lnvaluta, Gackstroemia
weindoiferi. Acrochila biserialis. Bazzania        monilinervis,    Lepidozia    ulothrix,
Lepidozia sp., ParaschislOchi/a lu/aides, Acromasligum mooreanum, Acromasligum
sp., Heteroscyphus cymbaliferus and Plagiochila circinalis . All the above moss
species are found on similar substrate in New Zealand and south-eastern mainland
Australia. The above hepatic species are found on similar substrate in New Zealand
but in Australia, P. tuloides, A. mooreanum and H. cymbaliferus are confined to
Tasmania.


The following descriptions provide the diagnostic features to allow the identification
of the key moss and hepatic epiphytes that occur on N. cunninghamii and Athrotaxis,

mosses possess papillae. The form of papillae occurring on these species is described
below.


Acrobolbus cinerascens (Acrobolbaceae)
The observations with the LM reveal little detail of the densely papillose surface
which is obscured especially by the underlying brownish cellular oil bodies (Fig.
5. 1a). A cross section seen under the LM (Fig. 5. 1b,c) shows that each cell is
covered on both surfaces by many stalked papillae which are extensions of the cell
wall material. These observations are confirmed when examined with the ESEM in
wet mode. The dorsiventral shoot (Fig. 5. ld) with its bilobed leaf (Fig. S . l e) have
numerous papillae which are clustered above the cell lumina (Fig. 5 . 1 f). The papillae
have a distinctive structure in that each is at the tip of a wall extension in the form of a
stalk which may be bifurcated (Fig. S. lg). The papillae are on average 4 to 5 ).lm
across and are covered by waxy deposits (Fig. 5.1g,h).

Frullania ferdinandi-muelleri Stephani (1910: 417)
PROTO: 'Australia, New South Wales.'
LABEL: NEW SOUTH WALES: Tintenbar(?), R[ichmond] R[iver], tea tree bark, W. W. Watts
153,2 Aug 1900.
TYPES: G (18211 ex Hb Levier 2628), NSW.
COMMENT: No information on locality, date, collector or specimen number(s) appears
in the protologue but Hattori (1979a) describes this species as known only from the
type collection and cites G 18211 as the type.

Frullania filipendula Stephani (1910: 419)
PROTO: 'Australia, Queensland, New South Wales.'
LABEL: NEW SOUTH WALES: E Ballina, base of saplings and on ground, heath, W. W. Watts
468, 17 June 1902 [2 packets in folder].
TYPES: G (18212 ex Hb Levier 3416), NSW.
COMMENT: No information on locality, date, collector or specimen number(s) appears
in the protologue but Bonner (1965: 304) cites this specimen (G 18212) as the type.
Seems to be conspecific with F. rubella (Hattori 1979a: 340).

W.W. W[atts] 62, 27 July 1911 [2 packets in folder].
TYPES: G (18215), NSW.
COMMENT: Endemic, known only from the type collection (Hattori 1979a: 346).

MARCHANTIOPHYTA_PHYLOGENY_2009  (1) 
Kuwahara (1978) himself recognises as belonging to Metzgeria. Although Schuster
                      (1992) also proposes reduction of Steereella to Metzgeria, this taxon differs enough
                      in morphology to warrant recognition until its status can be tested with molecular
                      data. The transfer of Vandiemenia to the Metzgeriaceae is supported by the mor-
                      phological studies of Furuki & Dalton (2008), and the reduction of Cryptothallus to
                      Aneura is based on the molecular analyses of Wickett & Goffinet (2008).
                         The Jungermanniidae are estimated to have diverged from the Metzgeriidae in the
                      Late Carboniferous (Heinrichs et al., 2007) and are today the most speciose subclass
                      of liverworts. As discussed in several publications (Davis, 2004; Heinrichs et al.,
                      2005, 2007; Forrest et al., 2006; He-Nygrén et al., 2006), Jungermanniidae are
                      generally resolved to comprise two major lineages, designated as Porellales and
                      Jungermanniales by Heinrichs et al. (2005). The majority of taxa that have been
                      included in various analyses clearly distribute between these two groups, an
                      exception being those of Ptilidiineae. In some analyses (He-Nygrén et al., 2004,
                      2006; Heinrichs et al., 2005, 2007), Ptilidiineae is aligned with Porellales, and in
                      others (Davis, 2004; Forrest et al., 2006), it resolves as the first divergence of
                      Jungermanniales, but neither placement is robustly supported. Recognising the
                      unique assemblage of porelloid and jungermannioid characters displayed by the taxa
                      currently resolved in this lineage, we have relegated Ptilidiineae to its own order, the
                      Ptilidiales. Although backbone relationships of the Jungermanniidae are fairly well
                      established, many generic and family level relationships are yet to be tested, since
                      molecular analyses have included slightly less than 25% of the genera comprising this
                      subclass. Consequently, we have relied heavily on morphology for placement of
                      genera in families, with molecular evidence primarily influencing the arrangement of
                      families in suborders.
                         Three suborders are recognised in the Porellales. This arrangement is compatible
                      with the analyses of Forrest et al. (2006) and is supported by morphological evi-
                      dence. As herein defined, all taxa of the Jubulineae are characterised by sporophytes
                      borne in stalked, true calyptrae and have beaked perianths, spheroidal capsules, and
                      vertically aligned elaters that are attached to the capsule valve apices. The Radu-
                      lineae lack underleaves, produce only Radula-type vegetative branches and form
                      stem perigynia below their flattened, truncate perianths. The Porellineae is more
                      heterogeneous, due primarily to the inclusion of the Lepidolaenaceae in the suborder.
                      This grouping is, nonetheless, supported by several molecular analyses (Heinrichs
                      et al., 2005, 2007; Forrest et al., 2006).
                         Generic level delineations in the Porellaceae, including the reduction of Macvicaria
                      to Porella, are based on the studies of Hentschel et al. (2007a), and the classification
                      of the Lejeuneaceae incorporates the molecular analyses of Wilson et al. (2007a).
                      As first suggested by Mizutani (1961) and later supported by several molecular
                      studies (Ahonen et al., 2003; Ahonen, 2004; Wilson et al., 2004, 2007a, 2007b),

                 Nipponolejeunea, which shares several features of sporophyte anatomy with Jubula,
                 is placed in the Jubulaceae and the Jubulaceae is recognised as distinct from both the
                 Frullaniaceae and the Lejeuneaceae, whereupon Lejeuneaceae Cavers becomes the
                 correct name for this taxon. Lejeuneaceae was nomenclaturally superfluous when
                 published by Cavers (1910–1911) because it included the type genus of the older
                 legitimate Jubulaceae H.Klinggr. (Klinggräff, 1858). Grolle (1973) proposed to
                 conserve Lejeuneaceae Casares-Gil, Fl. Ibér. Brióf.: 703 (1919), which was accepted
                 and afterwards included in Appendix II (Nomina Familiarum Conservanda) of the
                 Leningrad Code (Stafleu et al., 1978). That entry can be found in all subsequent
                 Codes until the recent Vienna Code (McNeill et al., 2006) where it was replaced by
                 the earlier published Lejeuneaceae Rostovzev, Morfol. Sist. Pechen. Mkhov: 94
                 (1913). However, according to Art. 52.3 of the Code (McNeill et al., 2006) since

                     SUBORDER: RADULINEAE R.M.Schust., J. Hattori Bot. Lab. 26: 229 (1963).
                     Plants irregularly pinnate to bipinnate, with branches of the Radula-type; leaves 2-lobed,
                     with the ventral lobule slightly inflated near the keel; underleaves absent; rhizoids in
                     fascicles from the leaf lobules; androecia amentiferous, on lateral branches, rarely
                     intercalary on the leading axis; gynoecia terminating a leading axis, rarely on a lateral
                     branch, with 2 to 4 archegonia; bracts in a single series; bracteoles absent; sporophytes
                     enclosed by a shoot calyptra or stem perigynium and perianth; perianths 2-keeled,
                     dorsiventrally compressed, with the mouth truncate; capsules cylindric, with the wall 2-
                     stratose, with both epidermal and inner cells bearing wall thickenings; multicellular discoid
                     gemmae in some species.
                        Radulaceae Müll.Frib., Lebermoose 1: 404 (1909). Radula Dumort. nom. cons.

                     the valve apices; spores with rosette markings in the exine (absent in Jubula).

                           Schusterella S.Hatt., Sharp & Mizut., and Steerea S.Hatt. & Kamim.]
                        Jubulaceae H.Klinggr., Höh. Crypt. Preuss.: 40 (1858). Leaves 2-lobed, with the dorsal
                           lobe margin spinose, rarely entire, ventral lobule forming water sacs of the Frullania-
                           type (Jubula) or Lejeunea-type (Nipponolejeunea), with a stalked slime papilla at the
                           postical insertion of the lobe in Jubula; underleaves bifid; vegetative branches mostly
                           of the Frullania-type; androecia spicate, on collared, Bryopteris-type branches, or
                           intercalary on a leading axis; gynoecia on leading axes, with 1 or 2 archegonia; bracts
                           and bracteoles in 1 to 3 series, with 1 or 2 Radula-type subfloral innovations; seta
                           4 cells in diameter, cruciate, nonarticulate; gemmae absent. Jubula Dumort. nom.

region, using the ‘track analysis’ method developed by Leon Croizat (Croizat 1964), and refined
by others (e.g. Grehan and Henderson 1988, Craw 1988).
Tangney (1989) noted that such studies are often limited by gaps in taxonomic treatments and
knowledge of the geographic distributions of taxa and of their evolutionary relationships. He
therefore considered only seven taxonomic groups known from New Zealand that had been the
subject of detailed investigations in the region: Dicnemonaceae, Hypnodendraceae,

                                              14
Hypopterygiaceae, Lembophyllaceae, Dawsonia, Hypnum, and Macromitrium. He concluded
that the moss distributions were consistent with other groups of organisms, supporting a general
pattern of local evolution of the regional biota rather than dispersal (i.e. immigration) from an
external centre of origin. He also identified five distinct distribution patterns involving five
tracks and six nodes (Figure 21.1). The tracks were:
• Track 1: southern Indonesia – Melanesia – New Caledonia – Vanuatu – Fiji
• Track 2: New Guinea – eastern Australia – Tasmania
• Track 3: central east Australia – Lord Howe Island – Norfolk Island – Vanuatu
• Track 4: New Zealand – New Caledonia – Solomon Islands
• Track 5: south-eastern Australia – Tasmania – New Zealand – southern South America
The nodes he identified were (1) New Guinea, (2) New Caledonia, (3) New South Wales –
Queensland border, (4) northern New Zealand, (5) Rapa Island, and (6) sub-Antarctic New
Zealand. Raoul Island, not mentioned by Tangney (1989), would lie along Track 4.
Numerous taxonomic treatments and floras relevant to the Tasman Sea region have been
published recently, so that a more comprehensive study of biogeographical relationships can
now be made. In relation to Lord Howe Island, we have wide-ranging taxonomic treatments and
geographic data for mosses (e.g. McCarthy 2006, Flora of Australia 2006, AMO 2016, AVH
2018), New Zealand (Beever et al. 1992, de Lange and Beever 2015, AVH 2018, Flora of New
Zealand 2014–2019), Norfolk Island (Streimann 2002) and New Caledonia (Thouvenot and
Bardat 2010), as well as liverworts and hornworts (e.g. McCarthy 2006, Engel and Glenny
2008, Thouvenot et al. 2011). There have also been major treatments of particular bryophyte
groups, such as those of Reese and Stone (1995) for Calymperaceae, So (2002) for Porella, So
(2003b) for Schistochila, Meagher (2008) for Bazzania and Renner et al. (2013) for Radula
subg. Odontoradula).
Further previous studies relating to specific aspects of biogeography are discussed in
Chapter 21.




Figure 21.1 Five tracks and six nodes identified by Tangney (1989, 2007).



                                               15
Lord Howe Island in the bryological literature2
The first published report of bryophytes from Lord Howe Island was made by Mitten (1873a,b),
who included seven mosses and one liverwort collected on the island by Milne and
MacGillivray. Hampe (1874), who described nine new moss species from the island, based
partly if not wholly on collections by Fullagar and Lind. Soon after, Jaeger (1878) published a
new species, Plagiothecium howeanum Müll.Hal. ex A.Jaeger, based on a collection by Fullagar
from Lord Howe Island. William Mitten (1882) listed 16 mosses from Lord Howe Island in his
catalogue of Australian mosses, comprising the nine species newly described by Hampe and the
seven mosses listed in Mitten (1873a).
In 1892 a list of 20 mosses collected on the island by Thomas Whitelegge in 1887 and identified
by Viktor Brotherus were listed in the Proceedings of the Linnean Society of New South Wales
as a result of their being exhibited by Whitelegge at a meeting of the society (Anon. 1892).
Seven of these were new species but were published without descriptions. Only two of these
seven were subsequently published with descriptions by Brotherus, leaving the other five as
nomina nuda with their identities unresolved.
In 1901 J.B. Förster described a new species, Rhacomitrium leptostomoides J.B.Först., from
Lord Howe Island, but the identity has been the subject of much uncertainty (see the discussion
in the taxonomic section on Grimmiaceae).

---

specimens, including two isolectotypes (NSW-433495, MEL-1038734), I agree with So’s
terminology. The underleaves and leaf lobules are not saccate in the usual sense of the term; the
margins are sometimes merely inrolled, never forming a bulging sac as in P. elegantula.

Porella elegantula (Mont.) E.A.Hodgs.
Madotheca elegantula Mont, Ann. Sci. Nat, Bot. sér. 2, 19: 255 (1843); Jungermannia elegantula (Mont.)
Hook.f. & Taylor, Bot. Antarct. Voy, 1, Fl. Antarct. 1: 163 (1845 [‘1844’]); Madotheca stangeri var.
elegantula (Mont.) Gottsche, Lindenb. & Nees, Syn. Hepat. 280 (1845); Porella elegantula (Mont.)
E.A.Hodgs., Svensk. Bot. Tidskr. 42: 277 (1948)



                                                                            191
Type: NEW ZEALAND, Auckland Islands, ‘in insulis Auckland invenit c. Hombron’; holotype
G, fide So (2002b), not seen; isotypes BM, FH (not seen).
Description: So (2002b).
Illustration: Hattori (1986, Figures 1, 2a–l); So (2002b, Figure 1c).
Habitat: Epiphytic, on trunks and branches of trees and shrubs in woodland, forest and
rainforest.
Distribution on LHI: Throughout the island.
Total distribution: Australia (NSW, Qld, LHI, NI), New Zealand (incl. Raoul I.).
Discussion: P. elegantula is easily distinguished from P. crawfordii by its much more robust
appearance and by the presence of distinct sacs on the basal margins of most if not all
underleaves and also commonly on the ventral base of the leaf lobules. It seems to be less
common on Lord Howe Island than P. crawfordii, but has a similar geographic distribution
there.


Radulaceae
Radula
The genus Radula is represented in Australia by at least 32 species (Renner 2005, McCarthy
2006, Renner et al. 2013), in the New Zealand botanical region by 23 species (Renner 2005),
and in New Caledonia by 8 species (Thouvenot et al. 2011). The genus is characterised by
flattened, spatula-shaped perianths, a leaf lobule consisting of a simply folded section of the
leaf, and a lack of underleaves. This is the first record of the genus from Lord Howe Island,
although three specimens from the island were in the NSW herbarium.

Radula novae-hollandiae Hampe ex Lehm.
Radula novae-hollandiae Hampe ex Lehm., Nov. Stirp. Pug. 7: 24 (1838); Stephanina novae-hollandiae
(Hampe ex Lehm.) Kuntze, Revis. Gen. Pl. 2: 839 (1891)
Type: AUSTRALIA, presumably New South Wales, ‘Crescit inter Filices in Nova Hollandia’,
F.W. Sieber s.n.; lectotype element C in S-B43474; isolectotypes element A in S-B43472,
apiculate element in S-B43471 ex herb. Lehmann; all fide Renner et al. (2013), not seen.
Description: Castle (1961), Renner et al. (2013).
Illustrations: Castle (1961, Figure 3); Renner et al. (2013, Figure 4).
Habitat: On rock, soil, rotting wood and tree or tree-fern trunks in damp to wet situations in
forest and rainforest.
Distribution on LHI: Southern hills and mountains.
Total distribution: Australia (NSW, Qld, LHI, NI), New Zealand (Raoul I.).
Discussion: The original description of Radula novae-hollandiae was based on a collection
made by F.W. Sieber, presumably in the Sydney area. It was placed in subgenus Odontoradula
by Renner et al. (2013), who revised the subgenus for Australasia.
No species of Radula has been reported previously from Lord Howe Island. The specimens seen
in this study, although sterile, are identified as R. novae-hollandiae on the basis of having acute
leaf apices, an indented corner to the leaf lobule in which a hyaline papilla is located, and
irregularly crenulate lobule margins.

                                                192
Radula novae-hollandiae has been reported from New South Wales, Queensland, Norfolk
Island and Raoul Island (Renner et al. 2013) so it is not surprising that it also occurs on Lord
Howe Island. However, it is surprising that only one species of Radula is known from Lord
Howe Island, despite suitable habitat being available. Perhaps there is a feature of Radula that
makes it unsuitable for long-distance dispersal, e.g. fragments might not survive the conditions
experienced at high altitudes in the atmosphere, or spore release might be infrequent or take
place when large storm systems are infrequent.


Lejeuneaceae Cas.-Gil
The family Lejeuneaceae is characterised generally by complicate bilobed leaves in which the
lobule is formed by the inrolling of the ventral leaf margin. Meagher (2008) outlined the
complex etymological history of the genera of Lejeuneaceae, which reflects the great confusion
about relationships in the family. About 30 genera of Lejeuneaceae are recognised from
Australia, New Zealand and New Caledonia (McCarthy 2006). Archilejeuna, Cheilolejeunea
and Mastigolejeunea have been reported from Lord Howe previously. Cololejeunea,
Leptolejeunea, Microlejeunea, Lejeunea, Lopholejeunea and Acrolejeunea are reported here as
new to the island.

PLAGIOCHILA_AUSTRALIA_2018  (1) 
in P. circumdentata, and P. kirkii or distributed all around the stem surface as in P. abietina or confined to the
ventral stem surface as in P. obtusa and P. streimannii. In Plagiochila abietina paraphyllia are usually uniseriate
projections, but larger paraphyllia comprising small flanges of tissue, sometimes bearing one or two teeth, are
also produced. Large branched and ciliate paraphyllia are produced by P. obtusa and P. streimannii; in both
these species paraphyllia are restricted to the ventral side of the stem. Paraphyllia in P. circumdentata and
P. kirkii are chartaceous, but in the latter some paraphyllia are long and approach lamellae. A single long linear
lamella is present on the dorsal stem surface adjacent each leaf insertion line in P. lamellata.
Asexual reproduction
Several modes of asexual reproduction occur in Australian Plagiochila species. The manifestation of these
various modes is more informative of broader relationships than species circumscription, nevertheless the
presence of a given type of asexual reproduction can often usefully localize an unknown plant to one or two
likely groups of species. Most species of sect. Vagae produce gemmae borne on the ventral surface of the leaves.
These form by budding, then proliferation of single leaf cells, and quickly assume the form of a small shoot.
They may be particularly dense in some individuals. Several species reproduce via caducous leaves, and these
may dislocate whole or in pieces. The leaves of P. minutissima and P. spinulosa dislocate as a whole from their
base, while the leaves of P. bantamensis dislocate as fragments starting at the leaf apex and working inward
toward the stem as the leaf ages.
Male bracts
The male bracts of Plagiochila exhibit useful variation. The male bracts in species of sect. Denticulatae are
remote to contiguous, and the bract lobe rolls down and sits below the next male bract, so stops below, an
arrangement called hypostatic. The other arrangement is exemplified by species of sect. Vagae whose male
bracts are imbricate, the bract lobe then laying upon the next male bract, so stopping on, an arrangement
called epistatic. Male bract lobes may be toothed or entire, and their apex rounded to acute, and some similar
species pairs may be readily distinguished on this basis, for example P. strombifolia has entire male bract lobes,
while P. fasciculata has dentate male bract lobes.
196                Telopea 21: 187–380, 2018                                                                                                                   Renner


                                                               Taxonomic Treatment
Plagiochila (Dumort.) Dumort. Recueil Observ. Jungerm. 14 (1835)
Basionym: Radula sect. Plagiochila Dumort., Syll. Jungerm. Europ. 42 (1831)
Type: Plagiochila asplenioides (L.) Dumort. Recueil Observ. Jungerm. 14 (1835)
Shoots in nearly all species divided into microphyllous basal stolon and leafy upper sector. Branching within
the stolon lateral- or ventral- intercalary. Vegetative branching within the leafy sector various, Frullania-type,
lateral-intercalary, or ventral-intercalary. Leaves succubously inserted, sometimes nearly transverse, usually
unlobed, rarely bifid, usually with toothed margins, teeth various. Underleaves usually reduced or absent, but
in sect. Cucullatae well developed, bifid, and ciliate. Stems with differentiated cortex, walls typically thickened
and brown pigmented. Dioecious. Androecia intercalary on shoots. Gynoecia terminal, on primary shoots
or any subordinate shoot order, in some species such as P. abietina on abbreviated shoots, with two leaf-like
bracts and sometimes a bracteole. Perianth laterally compressed, ventral face reduced or absent. Subfloral
innovations by lateral and/or ventral intercalary branching.


                                                     Key to Australasian Plagiochila.
1     Leafy shoots bipinnate, pinnate or irregular. Vegetative branching Frullania-type or
      lateral-intercalary, not pseudodichotomous. Asexual production, if present, by caducous leaves.
      Paraphyllia, if present, confined to dorsal stem surface or distributed on all stem surfaces. .....................13
1     Leafy shoots pseudodichotomous, vegetative branching Frullania-type. Asexual reproduction,
      if present, by gemmae produced from the leaf ventral surface. Paraphyllia, if present,
      confined to the ventral stem surface. ............................................................................................... sect. Vagae 2
2     Stems with paraphyllia on their ventral surface. ................................................................................................ 3
2     Stems without paraphyllia on their ventral surface. .......................................................................................... 4
3     Leaves falcate, with 2–7 triangular teeth around the apex. Underleaves conspicuous,
      larger than paraphyllia. . ...................................................................................................................P. streimannii
3     Leaves elliptic, with up to 25 spinose-ciliate teeth, sometimes on all margins.
      Underleaves similar in size to paraphyllia. . ........................................................................................... P. obtusa
4     Leaves on secondary, tertiary and quaternary shoots rectangular to narrowly-rectangular,
      apex bifid, often no other teeth present on leaf margin. ............................................................... P. daviesiana
4     Leaves on secondary, tertiary and quaternary shoots elliptic to ovate to ovate-triangular,
      apex truncate to rounded, with two or more teeth at apex and additional teeth on
      ventral leaf margin at least. ................................................................................................................................... 5

---

Recognition: Plagiochila ratkowskiana can be recognized by the combination of stolon production by ventral-
intercalary branching; vegetative leafy shoots originating by lateral-intercalary branching, the monomorphic
leafy shoots, and the transversely orientated, rotund leaves whose margins bear up to ten, occasionally more,
small triangular teeth (Fig. 9). Male plants are distinctive in their intercalary male branches bearing imbricate
male bracts that have entire margins, with androecia produced in close series along a shoot, separated by short
intervals of vegetative growth, and the occasional presence of androecial innovation by ventral-intercalary
branching. Also distinctive in the relatively (apparently) large leaf cells which impart a distinctive texture to the
dried plants, the broad cnemis, and the usually sparingly toothed leaf lobe margins. Depending on exposure
P. ratkowskiana can vary from forming short, tight turfs whose shoots bear leaves with at most two teeth near
the apex, or tall, openly branched mats whose shoots bear leaves with numerous small spinose-dentate teeth
along the postical margin. Female bracts and perianth mouths mirror the variation observed in leaf margins.
Plagiochila ratkowskiana could be confused with Cryptoplagiochila radiculosa (as in HO 577295), but
C. radiculosa has ovate leaves with a distinct bilobed apex, rather than subrotund leaves with margins entire
or with scattered triangular teeth. The perianths of C. radiculosa bear several triangular teeth at their apex and
have a basal shoot calyptra, whereas the perianths of P. ratkowskiana bear numerous small, short, spinose teeth
on the labia and do not have a basal shoot-calyptra.
Plagiochila ratkowskiana could be confused with Plagiochila fragmentissima, but P. fragmentissima has
asymmetrically ovate leaves, produces stolons by lateral-intercalary branching, and has triangular teeth on the
perianth labia.
Plagiochila ratkowskiana could be confused with P. deltoidea, but P. deltoidea has asymmetrically ovate leaves
with many sharp triangular teeth distributed around the apex and ventral margin, and perianth labia with large
dentate teeth.
Distribution and Ecology: Plagiochila ratkowskiana is endemic to Australia and occurs in Tasmania and on
Macquarie Island. In Tasmania Plagiochila ratkowskiana is a common liverwort, and grows as a lithophyte
or epiphyte in forests, grasslands and herbfields between sea level and more than 1000 m elevation. In forest
P. ratkowskiana may grow as a trunk or branch epiphyte on a range of tree species, including Nothofagus
and Acacia, with a wide range of other bryophytes including Cheilolejeunea spp, Cryptoplagiochila radiculosa,
Dinckleria pleurata, Frullania spp, P. strombifolia, Macromitrium spp, Orthotrichum spp, Zygodon spp. On
rocks P. ratkowskiana may form low or tall turfs depending on exposure, and may form pure or mixed turfs,
the latter with a wide range of bryophytes including Dinckleria pleurata, Frullania, Lepicolea scolopendra,
Macromitrium, Metzgeria, Radula multiamentula, and Syzygiella. In alpine habitats P. ratkowskiana may occur
on soil, or on exposed granite.

the ventral margin, usually with a gap along the ventral margin, in some leaves extending along the dorsal
margin, teeth similar stature around apex and base. Secondary shoot leaves oblong, 1090–2265 µm long ×
505–1170 µm wide, with a shallowly ampliate base, apex truncate, dorsal margin shallowly convexly curved
at its middle or, less commonly, straight; margins with 4–16 teeth distributed continuously or more often
discontinuously along the postical margin and apex with a gap at the middle of the ventral margin; tertiary
shoot leaves oblong, 900–1670 µm long × 330–730 µm wide, base not ampliate, apex truncate with two or three
teeth, dorsal margin shallowly convexly curved at its middle or straight; margins with 3–9 teeth irregularly
spaced along the ventral margin and around the apex, and occasionally onto the dorsal margin where one or
two teeth may be present near the apex; leaf insertion J-shaped, recurved at ventral end, decurrent dorsally,
attaining dorsal stem midline, not attaining the ventral stem midline, leaving two or more cortical cell rows
leaf-free. Marginal leaf cells quadrate to short rectangular, 14–21 µm long × 8–12 µm wide, long axis parallel or
perpendicular to leaf margin, walls with bulging to nodulose trigones but not continuously thickened; medial
leaf cells isodiametric to ovoid, 14–26 µm long × 12–20 µm wide, walls unpigmented, with bulging to bulging
and angular (block-like) trigones, medial thickenings absent, cells in leaf base polyhedral, slightly elongated,
24–49 µm long × 16–25 µm wide, walls with bulging to nodulose to block-like trigones, medial thickening
present or absent present on basal-most longitudinal walls, trigones and medial thickenings sometimes
confluent. Cell surfaces smooth. Oil-bodies not known. Underleaves present amid leaves at shoot apex, a tuft
of curved and branched cilia, disaggregating and dislocating, apparently absent from mature shoot sectors.
Asexual reproduction by leaf-borne propagules or leaf fragments absent.
Dioicous(?). Fertile material not seen. Fig. 35.
Recognition: The primary shoot leaves of Plagiochila alta are much longer than wide and distinctly oblong
in shape, except for the ampliate base. The ventral leaf margin is straight. The leaf margin bears 7–12(–14)
teeth that tend to cluster around the leaf apex and the ampliate basal portion of the ventral margin. However,
some leaves will be found in every specimen where teeth are evenly distributed along the entire ventral margin
between apex and base. As in P. arbuscula the teeth are more or less the same size at the apex and the base,
though those at the base do tend to be slightly longer.
Distribution and Ecology: Plagiochila alta occurs on a number of islands within the western Pacific, including
New Caledonia, Vanuatu, Samoa, Tahiti, Fiji and the Cook Islands. Plagiochila alta is also known, by a single
collection, within the New Zealand Botanical Region on Raoul Island in the Kermadec Islands. Here P. alta
grew with Radula javanica carpeting a damp boulder directly below the ephemeral waterfall, such that the
plants would be flooded during rain. On Rarotonga in the Cook Islands P. alta grew in root holes and crevices
under dense tangles of Freycinetia arborea. In Fiji Plagiochila alta has been collected growing as a trunk
epiphyte on a wide range of tree species and sizes, including saplings and mature canopy trees within wet
tropical rainforest between 400 and 1000 m elevation. For example along the southern sector of Namosi Road
it grew on sapling trunks on a steep slope with SE aspect in a gully head within mesophyll rainforest; and on
the summit of Nabukelevu mountain above Nadakuni Village it grew as an epiphyte on tree trunks mixed with
other bryophytes.

in outer half and straight at base, not ampliate; apex and ventral margins with 3–8 sharp triangular teeth; leaf
insertion J-shaped, recurved at ventral end, decurrent dorsally, not attaining dorsal stem midline, not attaining
the ventral stem midline, leaving two or more cortical cell rows leaf-free. Marginal leaf cells quadrate to short
rectangular, long axis parallel or perpendicular to leaf margin, 14–25 µm long × 12–20 µm, walls with bulging
trigones; medial leaf cells isodiametric to ovoid, 20–35 µm long × 17–30 µm wide, walls unpigmented, with
bulging trigones, and occasional medial thickenings, cells in leaf base ovoid, 22–45 µm long × 17–24 µm wide
walls with bulging trigones and occasional medial thickening, trigones not confluent. Cell surfaces smooth.
Oil-bodies not known. Underleaves absent. Asexual reproduction absent.
Dioicous. Androecia intercalary on shoots that continue vegetative growth, singly or in loose series, androecial
branching absent, Frullania-type, lateral-intercalary, and ventral-intercalary branches not associated with
male branches; bracts in 10–17 pairs, contiguous, bract-lobe reduced and smaller than adjacent leaves, with
3–6 teeth, epistatic, margin of dorsal lobe with prominent laciniate tooth, antheridia 1 per bract; stem among
bracts smooth. Gynoecia at shoot apices; bracts obovate, larger than adjacent leaves, 1620–1950 µm long ×
660–720 µm wide; female bracteole absent. One subfloral innovation produced by lateral-intercalary branching
from between the female bracts. Perianth campanulate, to 2000 µm long and 1300 µm wide at mouth, dorsal
keel longer than ventral keel, dorsal and ventral keels unwinged; laterally compressed, lateral walls plane; labia
curved, each with 10–15 laciniate teeth. Figs 39, 40.
Recognition: Plagiochila monospiris can be recognized by its small size relative to other Plagiochila species,
the leaves obovate in situ with 3–8 triangular teeth around their apex, and the persistent, not caducous, leaves.
Plagiochila monospiris differs from P. vernicosa by the paucity of branching within leafy shoot sectors, by the
more numerous, smaller and more evenly sized teeth on the leaf and female bract margins, and by the obovate,
rather than oblong to oblong-lanceolate leaf shape (Inoue 1972).
Plagiochila monospiris is also similar to P. minutissima, for characters differentiating these two species see the
recognition section of the latter, below.
Distribution and Ecology: Plagiochila monospiris occurs in Papua New Guinea and Australia. In Papua
New Guinea, P. monospiris was initially reported from Goodenough Island, on the northeastern side of the
mainland, and Rossel Island at the eastern end of the Louisade Archipelago, it has since been reported from
the Papua New Guinean mainland. In Australia Plagiochila monospiris is widely distributed through the Wet
Tropics Bioregion of north-east Queensland in tropical montane forests above 800 m from Mt Finnegan in
the north to the Paluma Range in the south. Plagiochila monospiris often grows on decaying logs on the forest
floor or on the bases and lower trunks of trees with other corticolous species including Acroscyphella, Radula
ornata, R. ocellata, and Telaranea spp.

RADULA_NZ_TASMANIA_2005  (1) 
J HalTori Bot. Lah. No. 97: 39- 79 (Jan. 2(05)




       ADDITIONS TO THE RADULA (RADULACEAE: HEPATICAE)
            FLORAS OF NEW ZEALAND AND TASMANIA

                                         MATT A. M. RENNERI


ABSTRACT. Radula javanica a widespread Melanesian-oceanic species is recorded from the Ker-
madec Islands, the northern most landmass within the New Zealand Botanical District. The speci-
mens upon which the New Zealand record of R. campanigera is based present themselves as an un-
usual taxon close to R. cordiloba, which is described as a new subspecies, R. cordiloba ssp. erigens
M. Renner et Braggins ssp. novo This taxon is known from only two collections from the Kermadec
Islands. Radula cordiloba ssp. erigens differs from R. cordiloba in its unique localisation of caducous
leaves on secondary branches which are determinate in growth, and the broadly ovate lobules that oc-
casionally extend beyond the opposite stem margin . Radula campanigera is accordingly excluded
from the New Zealand flora. The ranges of four more Radula species are extended to New Zealand:
R. aneurysmalis, R. compacta , R. ratkowskiana and R. tasmanica were previously considered endem-
ic to Tasmania; Radula tabularis is excluded from the New Zealand flora, specimens upon which this
record is based are referable to R. ratkowskiana and R. tasmanica. Radula sainsburiana is reported
new to Tasmania, and outside of New Zealand for the first time. Descriptions and illustrations of all
seven species discussed are provided, as is a key to species of Radula currently known for New
Zealand and Tasmania.
KEY WORDS: Radula, New Zealand, Tasmania, Kermadec Islands, new records, new subspecies, key.

                                                 INTRODUCTION
     Radula was first formally reported for New Zealand in 1843 with the description of R.
physoloba by Montagne (Montagne 1843) from material collected in New Zealand. This
was followed by several other new species published in the later part of the 19th and early
20th Centuries by Hooker & Taylor (1845), and by Stephani (1884, 1892, 1909). Hodgson
(1944) in the only study to date dealing specifically with the New Zealand taxa, recognised
a total of thirteen species as occurring in New Zealand. Verdoorn (1935) presented R. al-
stonii Castle ex Verdoorn, a typographical error and nomen nudum, which was later for-
malised as the intended R. allisonii by Castle (1962). Hodgson described two further
species, R. multiamentula E.A.Hodgs. (= R. scariosa Mitt. fide Yamada 1999) and R.
parviretis E.A.Hodgs., and recorded R. caespitosa Steph. for New Zealand (Hodgson 1962,
1965), bringing the number of species recognised in New Zealand to 16. At the end of Cas-
tles' 30 year monographic reign in 1967, 17 species were recognised for New Zealand.
Castle passed over the Radula sp. included by Chapman (1961) in his work on Kermadec
Island Bryophytes. Campbell (1977) added R. campanigera to the New Zealand flora based
on this material. Yamada (1984) placed R. mittenii into synonymy under R. buccini(era, ef-
fectively removing this species from the New Zealand flora.

  1 Auckland War Memorial Museum, Auckland, New Zealand. Current address: Institute of

Wildlife Research, School of Biological Sciences, Heydon-Laurence Building A08, University of
Sydney, Sydney, NSW 2006, Australia. Email: matthew.Renner@rbgsyd.nsw.gov.au.
40                                  J. Hattori Bot. Lab. No. 97                       2 0 0 5


      As a result of fieldwork in New Zealand, and study of herbarium material from New
Zealand and Tasmania, six species are recorded new to New Zealand, and one species as
new to Tasmania. All species reported here as new to New Zealand and Tasmania have
been collected previously by other workers. However, with the exception of R. aneUlys-
malis and R. fasmanica all the material collected beyond published known ranges has been
misidentified. These two species have been identified correctly from New Zealand material ,
by Schuster and Yamada respectively, though these records remain unpublished. Radula
aneUlys/11a!is was collected and identified independently, though I was first aware of the ex-
istence of R. tasmanica from correctly identified herbarium specimens. The record of R.
sainshuriana increases to 14 the number of Radula recorded for Tasmania (based on
Ratkowsky 1987). The six species here reported new to New Zealand increase the number
of Radula species found within the New Zealand botanical region (as defined in Allan
1961) to 23, an increase of 25 % (based on Glenny 1998). This is a reflection on how poorly
known the genus Radula has been in New Zealand, and indeed Tasmania, particularly when
species can have their ranges significantly extended simply through cursory examination of
a representative range of inter-loaned herbarium material.
      Raduta has something of a reputation for being a difficult group among the Hepaticae
(Yamada 1979), which may go some way to explaining the lack of recent work in this
genus, and therefore the current state of its knowledge in New Zealand. Perhaps, given
some of the confusion outlined here, and later in this work, workers of the genus them-
selves must accept part of the responsibility for this perception, inadequate descriptions
and poor illustrations forming the literary foundation upon which regional study must be
based (see for example Hodgson 1944; Castle 1967; Oliveira de Silva & da Costa Feitosa
1997). Unlike regions such as South Africa (Jones 1977), Asia (Yamada 1979), North
America (Schuster 1980a), and India (Udar & Kumar 1982, 1983) New Zealand has never
been provided with an adequate foundation upon which regional study may proceed. This
is reflected in the plethora of specimens lodged in local herbaria simply as Radula sp. and
the number of misidentifications present in collections identified to species level. In recog-
nition of current confusion regarding the identification of species, full descriptions and il-
lustrations of the species discussed and a key to all species known for New Zealand and
Tasmania are presented.

                                 MATERIALS AND METHODS

      Field collected material from a range of localities in New Zealand's North , South and
Stewart Islands, as well as specimens from AK, AKU (now incorporated into AK), BM ,
CHR, CANS, E, F, HO herbaria were examined. All field collected material is deposited in
AK. Descriptive statistics were obtained by capturing a digitized image using either SEM
or light microscopy and using the program NIH image 1.62 to measure the structure of in-
terest. Material was cleared prior to investigation by mounting in Hoyers solution. Charac-
ters associated with the cell wall anatomy of the sporophytes capsule valves were examined
using techniques and procedures described by von Konrat et al. ( 1999), with minor modifi-
cation (Renner & Braggins in prep). Spore and elater descriptions were based on SEM mi-
crographs. Measurements are reported as minimum and maximum values recorded for rep-
                            M. A. M. RENNER: New austral Radllla records                        41


resentative ranges of material examined.
      Both fresh and herbarium material was used in SEM investigation. Herbarium materi-
al was rehydrated for 24 hours before freeze-drying, fresh material freeze-dried directly.
Material was placed within a six-compartment histological cassette (Tissue-Tek) and snap-
frozen in liquid nitrogen, then vacuum-dried immediately to a Tor of I X 10- 2 • Freeze-dried
material was then mounted onto stubs covered in conductive carbon pads, which were then
coated with a 2- lOnm layer ofa 50-50 gold and palladium mix, in a Photron X7640 sput-
ter coater. Specimens were examined with a Philips X86C EE.G. scanning electron micro-
scope, with an accelerating voltage of 5 kYA.

Description olmorphologicalfeatures
     Lobule description follows Jones (1977), who devised a scheme that could be applied
consistently across the entire range of variation in lobule architecture within the genus. I
employ a similar scheme to the description oflobes and lobules, as follows.
     Lobe dimensions (Fig. 1) given as I) length, 2) length above keel, 3) overall width, 4)
width exterior to keel, and 5) insertion length; to describe the basic aspects of lobe con-
struction, 6) length above stem insertion, anchoring and orientating each of these dimen-
sions with the stem. Seven lobule dimensions (Fig. 2) are measured, all being made either
parallel or perpendicular to the keel. The measurements used are I) the stem insertion, 2)
the height of the free antical margin from the top of the stem insertion, perpendicular with



     1.               3                                                    3


                                         6



                                               2
              4                      5                                                      1




                                                                               5

                                                                                     3.
        Leaves of Radula (Figs. 1- 3). I. Lobe measurement axes. 2. Lobule orientating points
   and axes, following lanes (1977). 3. Female bract measurement axes.
42                                          1. Hattori Bot. Lab. No. 97                                    2 0 0 5


the keel, 3) the free interior margin , 4) the lobule depth, 5) the lobule breadth, 6) the length
of the free exterior margin, 7) the keel length . Female bract lobes (Fig. 3) however are dif-
ferent, as the stem is not able to be used to anchor the descriptions. We use the same di-
mensions as in measurement of the lobe, but orientate the measurements with the stem in-
sertion of the bract. Female bract lobules are measured in same way as vegetative lobules,
as the keel anchors meas urement.

                   ARTIFI C IAL K EY TO R ADULA OF NEW ZEALAND AND T ASMAN IA

      Wherever possibl e lobules from leaves immediately preceding gynoecia should be
used for identification purposes, as these shoot sectors are typically well developed, and ro-
bust. Lobules from 'weaker' branches may not express diagnostic features , this may lead to
confusion. The key is aimed at herbarium material, which is often sterile, so is based large-
lyon gross morphologica l features, details of oil-bodies and perianths are utilised where
convenient, or useful for identifying fresh material , though are not essential. For conve-
nience and ease of use we have tried to avoid anatomical features as a source of definitive
characters. The purpose of the key is to facilitate easy accurate identification of Radula
spec ies, rather than to be reflective of relationships .
      I have been unable to include R. wa ffsiana in thi s key as I have not examined material
I am confident is thi s species, and have not seen the type. I refer readers to Castle (1963)
for differences from R. buccinijera, to where this species should key.

Key to Radula cordiloba subspecies
I.    Cauducous leaves produced infrequently on all stems, lateral branches indeterminate. Lobules on
      main shoots with exterior free margin angular [Oceanic Southern Pacific] .
       . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . Radula cordiloba ssp. cordiloba.
I.    Caducous leaves produced only on lateral branches, which are determinate in length giving the
      shoots a fish-bone appearance. Lobules on main shoots with exterior free margin evenly rounded
      [Kermadec Islands]. ..................................... Radula cordiloba ssp. erigens

Radula cordiloba ssp. erigens M .Renner et Braggins ssp. novo HOLOTYPE: Raoul Island,
1956, R. Cooper, (AK44347, ex AKU063 120).
      Diagnosis: Subspecies nova ab Radula cordiloba ssp. cordiloba ramificatione regu-

[Branching regularly pinnate, lateral branches determinate in length becoming naked by the
fragmentation of caducous leaves, giving the shoots an appearance similar to a fish skele-
ton which, with the even ly rounded imbricated lobules that often exceed the opposite stem
margin, distingui sh thi s taxon from R. cordiloba ssp. cordiloha]
       Description (Figs. 4- 15): Plants forming extensive pure mats, olive-brown, with
go lden tint in herbari a. Pl ants large, shoots to 2800 pm wide, 25 mm or more long, regular-
ly pinnately branched, the subordinal branches alternate, separated by at least one branch
free leaf pair, arising at an angle of 70- 80 degrees to the ordinal axis, to 3- 5 mm long, ter-
minating after a period of growth. Lobes only rarely fragmenting from the ordinal axes, al-
though caducous leaves become more common as the stem ages, leaf-lobes on subordinal
branches are consistently caducous, so that the branches appear leaftess, except for 2- 3
new ly produced leaves at the branch tip, lateral branches that have suspended growth lac k
leaf lobes entirely, shoots thus with a herring-bone appearance . Microphyllous and amentu-
lose branches absent. Stems c. 200 pm diameter, 10- 12 cell rows across, oval in cross sec-
tion . Cortical cells rectangular, in one distinct row, medullary cells irregular, rounded. All
cell walls thickened at ang les by isoscehedral trigones, slightly bulging on all sides, other-
wise cell walls unthickened, except where trigones tend to coalesce. Free external wall
thickened to the same degree as the internal walls. Lobes dense ly imbricate, rectangular,
the antical and postical margins running perpendicular to the stem direction, apex broadly
rounded, exterior margin slightly inrolled, interior margin extending well beyond the oppo-
site stem margin , the lobes imbricated so the stem is entirely obscured in dorsal view.

2-4 cells wide. Lobules broadly ovate, interior margin often reaching and occasionally
overlapping the opposite stem margin, contiguous so that the stem is virtually obscured in
ventral view. Keel emerging at an angle of 45 degrees from the stem, straight for most its
length, then turning outward, the very end becoming perpendicular to the stem. Insertion
longitudinal, climbing gradually onto the stem , almost reaching the stem midline, but leav-

where they rise over the stem. Rhizoids infrequently produced from approximately the cen-

unornamented. Oil-bodies not seen. Asexual reproduction by caducous leaves, the pattern

      Additional specimens examined: Radu/a cordi/oba ssp. erigens: Raoul Island, 1956,
R. Cooper AK44340 (ex AKU 063117); R. cordi/oba ssp. cordiloba, in insulis pacificis, ex
Herb. W Wilson (BM - isotype material ?); Austral Islands, Ruruta, near Taepe and Teti,
310 m, H. St. John, Aug 1934, (F 1361583).
      Distribution and habitats: This subspecies is currently known from two collections
both made on Raoul Island in the Kermadec group. It is possible, though unlikely, that this
species reaches far northern mainland New Zealand or other offshore islands such as the
Three Kings. No information regarding the species ecology is recorded with the herbarium
specimens. Plants from both collections have stems of Fissidens hyophilus Mitt. (kindly de-
termined by Or. Jessica Beever) scattered through them. Fissidens hyophilus grows on a
wide range of substrates, including live bark, tree trunks, roots, and trunks of Rhapolosfy/is
sapida (Nikau Palm), and also on rock including greywacke and rhyolitic basalt (J. E.
Beever pers. comm.). The remnant substrate adhering to the specimens suggest both were
collected from ledges or crevices on a basaltic rock face under a forest canopy, where re-
cently eroded basalt had accumulated.
      Recognition: The key distinguishing features of this species are the consistent pro-
duction of caducous leaves on the subordinal axes, and the broadly ovate lobules that occa-
sionally extend beyond the opposite stem margin. In these two characters, R. cordi/oba ssp.
erigens resembles no other Raduta known from the New Zealand region.
      Radu/a cordiloba ssp. erigens is distinguished from R. cordiloba ssp. cordi/oba by its
evenly rounded, imbricated lobules, and the presence of caducous leaves which are, unique-
ly, localised on lateral branches of determinate length. The two subspecies are allopatric, R.
cordiloba ssp. erigens known only from the Kennadec Islands, while R. cordiloba ssp.
cordiloba is widespread on islands of the Oceanic Pacific.
      Raduta cordiloba can be confused with R. javanica. However in R. javanica the lob-
ules of the ordinal axes are sub-rhomboidal with an acute apex, and the interior margin of
                            M. A. M. RENN ER: New austral Radula records                     49

which never extends beyond the stem midline. R. cordiloba ssp. erigens is further distin-
guished by I) the subordinal axes suspending growth at between 3 and 5 millimetres in
length ; and 2) the branching being regularly, rather than irregularly pinnate. Radula
cordiloba is distinguished from R. campanigera by I) the stem cortical cells lacking heavi-
ly thickened cell walls which restrict the cell lumen; 2) the insertion of the lobules being
longitudinal , rather than virtually transverse; 3) the interior free margin of the lobule being
approximately equal to, or just slightly less than the length of the stem insertion, rather
than much greater to it; 4) medial leaf cells with small concave trigones rather than large
nodular trigones that bulge on all sides at the cell angles; and 5) the lobes being imbricate
to densely imbricate rather than contiguous to slightly remote.
      Notes: In \956, an expedition lead by R. C. Cooper, then Auckland Institute and
Museum (AK) botanist, was undertaken to the Kermadec Islands in order to compile a
species list for the islands (P. J. de Lange pers. comm.). Collections of vascular plants and
cryptograms, including some hepatics, were brought back to Auckland for investigation.
While it was intended that all material be lodged in AK, the material was deposited in
AKU after being given AK accession numbers, hence the apparently confused doubling of
specimen numbers. It is from one of these collections that R. campanigera Mont. came to
be reported for New Zealand (Campbell 1977). Two packets determined are present in AK
(ex AKU), and are the only material of this species collected within the New Zealand
Botanical Region (as defined in Allan 1961). The material in question has an interesting
history. The collections were lodged with the Auckland University Herbarium (AKU), and
have been identified variously as firstly Radula sp. (Chapman 1961) and then Radula ja-
vanica, (identifier unknown) and R. campanigera (Campbell 1977) and finally Radula sp.
by Yamada (nom. herb.). However, the specimens in question are clearly not Radula cam-
panigera, nor any species allied to it in that the plant is not a member of subgenus Clado-
radula Spruce, to which R. campanigera belongs (see Renner & Braggins 2004, in press).
      We have been unable to obtain fresh or fertile material for study due to the remote and
relatively inaccessible nature of the island group where this species in known to occur.
      The rank of subspecies has been chosen in preference to species, because aside from
the unique localisation of caducous leaves, and the broadly rounded lobule apices, this
taxon is very similar to the allopatric Radula cordiloba .

western coast of the North and South Islands, and on Stewart Island. Probably far more
common than the limited number of collections cited here suggest. Radula aneurysmalis
has been collected frequently from the bark of Leptosperl11ul11 scoparium and Kunzea spp.
in well lit situations. Radula anue/ysl11alis often grows alone on thi s substrate, forming

thread like mats. This habit is reminiscent of Drepanolejeunea which is superficially simi-
lar to R. aneurysmalis. Radula aneurysmalis often grows among other byophytes, particu-
larly R. £lvi/era, R. tasmanica, Heteroscyphus sp. and Frullania spp. Radula aneurysmalis
also grows amongst moss and other hepatics in crevices in rock at high altitudes.
      Recognition: Radula aneurysmalis is distinct in its extremely small size, with shoots
less than 350 pm wide, and in its subequal lobes and lobules.
      Radula aneurysmalis is superficially similar to smaller members of the Lejeuneaceae,
such as Drepanolejeunea and Harpalejeunea , however is immediately identified by its I)
lack of underleaves, 2) production of rhizoids on the ventral surface of the lobule, never on
the stem, 3) the single large oil body per lobe cell, and 4) the dorso-ventrally flattened peri-
anths, that are never inflated.
      Radula aneurysmalis can be confused with R. physoloba, as extensive growth of mi-
crophyllous branches can occur in R. physoloba, particularly when plants of the latter grow
above tree line. However, these branches can always be seen to originate from the large full
sized axes they grow with, and they never produce antheridia or gynoecia. Production of
reproductive organs from microphyllous branches is indicative of R. aneurysmalis. Radula
aneurysmalis can also be distinguished from microphyllous axes of R. physoloba by a
number of features which are summarised in Table I.
      Radula aneUly smalis bears a particular resemblance to R. compacta, best expressed in
the form of the lobules preceding the gynoecia. These two species are separable only by the
persistent microphyllous habit of R. aneurysmalis, and the association of microphyllous
axes with axes of normal stature in R. compacta.
      Notes: While Schuster (1980b) uses the spelling R. aneurismalis, we follow the
spelling presented in Taylor's protologue. Rodway (1916) was of the opinion that R. tas-
manica was a well developed form of R. aneurysmalis. Most likely Rodway was confusing
R. tasmanica with R. physoloba or R. compacta (see under R. tasmanica below) as differ-
ences in the oil-bodies between these two species (see below) clearly indicate that this can-
not be the case, besides which R. tasmanica never produces microphyllous branches. As
pointed out above, this species does bear a marked resemblance to the microphyllous axes
of R. physoloba, and an even closer resemblance, particularly in the form of the lobule on
leaves preceding the production of gynoecia, to R. compacta. Radula aneurysmalis may be
a neotenic derivative of this species, however the affinities of these species need clarifying.

apex when well developed, and has a pronounced ampliate base, 2) the single, or rarely two

       Distribution and habitats : In New Zealand known from three localities, Rocky
Mountain on Stewart Island, and Secretary Island and Wet Jacket Arm within Fiordland
National Park. At Rocky Mountain, this species is common on the lower trunks of mature
Leptospermum scoparium , within the short Olearia colensoi communities (mutton bird
scrub) that occur near the tree line where it grows with Hymenophy llum armstrongii, Acro-
mastigum mooreanum , Bazzania monolinervis, Heteroscyphus menziesii, H. circumdentata,
Frullania ptycantha, Radula tasmanica, R. uvifera, Harpalejeunea latitans, Drepanoleje-
unea aucklandica and other bryophytes . Radula ratkowskiana also grows conspicuously
alone on the trunks and branches of Dracophyllum longifolium in the same scrub commu-
nity. On Secretary Island R. ratkowskiana was collected from the trunk of Podocarpus hal-
hi in forest at 300 metres altitude. At Wet Jacket Arm this species was collected growing
over bryophytes on a small rock face within Notho{agus menziesii - N. solandri var. cliffor-
tiodes forest canopy on treeline.
      Recognition: The presence of dome shaped papillae over the entire surface of the
lobes is nearly unique within Radula, being shared only with R. ornata E.A.Brown et Pocs
(Brown & Pocs 200 I), and is thus exceptionally di stinctive . The manner in which the labia
of the perianth roll inwards appears similarly unique, as does the occurrence of heteroge-
nous oil-bodies within the same cell. This species is also distinctive in its colour and eco-
logical preference, being a bright green, and, within its known New Zealand range, occur-
ring primarily on the trunks of Dracophy llum longifolium .

a collection made by Dr. G. A. M. Scott on Secretary Island (OTA 609249) having " notice-
ably papillose cells .. . that is presumably this species". While Hodgson's observations were
accurate, her interpretation was not, this material is the earliest known collection of R.

       Distribution and habitats: Known in Tasmania to date from only Mt. Field and above
Lake Skinner. This species is widespread, though not abundant in wet alpine regions
throughout Southern New Zealand, and extends at least as far north as Mt. Ruapehu . This
species grows on damp rock and soil at high altitude, and occurs in this habitat at all known
locations.
       Notes: Radula sainsburiana has been considered endemic to New Zealand, this is
the first report for this species outside New Zealand. Material belonging to this species was
recognised from a representative range of specimens of Radula taxa interloaned from Ho-
bart (HO). Both collections had been misidentified, one attributed to R. wattsiana by
Stephani , the other to R. tasmanica, by Yamada.
       Recognition: Radula sainsburiana is unique among the New Zealand and Tasman-
ian taxa in its paroicious habit. It is readily recognisable in its small size, bright yellow
colouration, and occurrence on soil and rock at high altitudes. The lobule, in which the
apex barely exceeds the level of the top of the stem insertion in its normal arrangement in
vivo, due to the way in which the antical margin is inrolled, is also distinctive . The two oil
bodies, which are smooth, colourless, and contain an internal, spherical hyaline body, or
'eye-drop' are also unique.

Radula tasmanica Steph. Spec. Hep. 4: 212 (1909). TYPE: Tasmania, Williamsford, leg. W
A. Weymouth 1906. E. Levier No. 5779, in Hb. Steph. (G)! [Lectotypified by Castle (1963 ,
p. 38)]
      Description (Figs. 88- 102): Plants forming close set mats of descending, overlap-
ping stems, mid green, tending yellow green in exposed situations. Shoots to 1000 f.1m
wide, branching pinnate in male plants, and predominantly pseudodichotomous in female
plants. Microphyllous and amentulose branching absent. Stems to 60 pm in diameter, 5- 6
cell rows across, the cells 6.8-9.5 pm diameter, ovoid, cortex and medullary cells similar in
size, not architecturally differentiated, internal cell walls lacking secondary thickening, ex-
cept at the angles, which bear small, though distinct trigones concave on all sides, free ex-
ternal wall not, or only marginally thicker than the internal cell walls . Lobes ovate, typical-
ly inrolled along a line extrapolated from the curvature of the keel. Lobes inclined at c. 20
degrees to the stem, imbricated, antical margin of distal lobule overlapping up to half the
length of the next lobule, stem usually obscured in dorsal view, insertion of the lobe runs
alongside the stem, then abruptly rides up onto the stem, giving the insertion an inverted J
shape in dorsal view, the stem insertion reaching, but not crossing the stem midline, leav-
ing no dorsal leaf free strip. Dimensions: Length 605- 740 pm, length above keel
350--450 pm, overall width 560- 705 pm, width exterior to keel 165- 250 pm. Lobules rec-
tangular in general appearance, though free margins slightly convex, the apex obtuse, keel
strongly convexly arched, angle between stem and keel apex 44- 62 °, carinal region strong-
ly inflated, lobe-lobule join consistently notched (apparent when flattened) , lobule attached
72                                        J. Hattori Bot. Lab. No. 97                                 2 0 0 5




      88

---

walls. Cell surfaces smooth. Oil bodies (2-)3-5 per cell, ellipsoid 5.3- 7.2 X3. 1- 5.4 pm,
arranged in a ring around the margin of the cell , surface smooth, colourless, internally ho-
mogenous except for a central spherical colourless body. No asexual reproduction observed
in any of the material studied.
      Dioicious. Androecia with 1- 2 pairs of bracts on subordinal branches, which are in-
determinate, often 2 or 3 androecia occur in sequence on a single branch, each separated by
2- 3 pairs of vegetative leaves. Androecial bracts imbricate, narrower than the vegetative
lobes, outline similar, lobule outline similar, though the keel strongly arched, the apical
portion parallel with the stem. Gynoecia borne terminally on ordinal axes, consistently
with two subfloral innovations that grow on and fructificate themselves. Archegonia
152- 164 pm. Female bracts in one pair only, with insertion lines interlocking. Lobe spathu-

keel length 325- 375 pm, free exterior margin 335- 385 pm, Perianths to 2500 pm long by
950 pm wide at the mouth, tubular at the base, flaring into compressed distal portion,
widest at the mouth, which is sometimes notched . Capsules obovoidal, to 1000 pm
long X450 pm wide, capsule walls bistratose, epidermal cells rectangular, with thickening
occuring as discrete close-set nodules, or as confluent thickening appearing composed of
melded nodular thickenings and covering entire longitudinal wall surface. Confluent thick-
ening common every second cell wall, interspersed with discrete nodules. Thickening on
alternate longitudinal walls less dense, comprising scattered discrete nodular thickenings,
occasionally of lesser stature. Thickening absent from transverse walls. Inner layer with
rectangular to V-shaped cells, rather irregularly arranged, some rectangular cells contain
isolated internal cell walls. Longitudinal and transverse cell walls bear a thin layer of con-
tinuous secondary thickening interspersed with isolated small nodular thickenings, that do

not run onto the internal tangential cell wall, and which aggregate at the cell angles. Valve
outer surface smooth, inner surface bearing fine granular ornamentation evenly distributed
evenly over surface. Elaters 142-218 f.1m long, 6.2- 9.2 f.1m wide at middle, then long taper-
ing to each end, giving elaters a very narrow rhomboidal appearance, bispiral, looped at

      Specimens of Radula tabu/aris examined: SOUTH AFRICA: Cape Province, Table Mountain,
 Pillaus 4226, E00111868 ; Cape Province, Deepwell forest, 23 .11.1951 , S. W Arnell 1532
 (EOO 111867); Cape province, Table Mountain, east side, wormhole ravine, 2500-3000 ft, 19.2.1956,
 E. Esterhuysen 25363 (EOII1869).
       Distribution and habitats: Widespread in the south, where it occurs from sea level to
 treeline growing corticolously in forest particularly on the trunks of Weinmannia racemosa
 in well lit situations in wet forest, where it grows with Radula uvifera, Heterosypus m en-
 ziesii, Bazzania spp. The species is rarer in the North Island, where, travelling northward,
76                                   1. Hattori Bot. Lab. No. 97                          200 5


its occurrence is increasingly restricted to the cloud forests of higher elevation mountain
peaks, particularly in the upper half of the island i.e. Wharauroa on Mt Pirongia, in the
Waikato, Kiriraukawa, Orau, and Hauturu on Little Barrier Island, and Hauturu in the
Waima Range, Northland.
      Differentiation: Radula tasmanica can be distinguished from other Radula species
by the combined occurrence of a number of characters I) When fresh , its mid green
colouration, the plants usually lack brown, orange and yellow tints, although plants from
exposed situations may be difficult to separate from R. uvifera by colour alone. 2) The de-
scending growth habit, which in vigourous plants tends to aggregate the stems into a mat.
3) The complete absence of amentulose and microphyllous branching. 4) The perianths
without plicae. 5) The obtuse lobe apex. 6) The interior lobule margin attached to the stem
for its entire length . 7) The 2- 5 small, ellipsoid oil bodies in each lobe cell, that contain a
spherical hyaline internal body, or 'eye-drop'. The oil-bodies of R. tasmanica are highly
distinctive, and are useful for identification, as R. plicata and R. sainsburiana are the only
other New Zealand species with similar oil-bodies. Radula tasmanica is readily distin-
guished from R. plicata by I) the lobe cell walls having (usually) distinct concave trigones
at their angles, where the walls of R. plicata lack any thickening, 2) the lobes not falcate,
compared to the strongly falcate lobes of R. plicata , 3) the lobules being rectangular, rather
than rhomboidal in outline, 4) the exterior portion of the lobe inrolled along a line extrapo-
lated from the keel, giving the lobe the appearence of a baseball glove, whereas the lobe in-
rolled along a line parallel to the stem, or not at all in R. plicata, 5) the absence of plicae on
the perianth, as opposed to the conspicuous plicae of R. plicata. From R. sainsburiana, R.
tasmaniccl can be distinguished by its I) larger size, 2) dioicous condition, and 3) rectangu-
lar lobules.
      In some situations, R. tasmanica bears a striking resemblence to R. uVljera, especially
where the two grow together in high light situations. However, R. lasmanica is easily dis-
tinguished by I) the complete absence of amentulose branching, as opposed to amentulose
branches usually produced every second leaf pair, although they may also be infrequently
produced and widely spaced in some plants of R. uvi{era, 2) the oil bodies which are small,
2- 5 per cell, opposed to the single, large coarsely botryoidal oil body of R. uvifera cells 3)
the absence of lamellae on the perianth, whereas R. uvifera bears 3- 14 rows of lamellae on
each surface of the perianth.
      Radula tasmanica can be distinguished from R. physoloba by I) the lack of any yel-
lowish colouration, opposed to the strong golden yellow green of R. physoloba, 2) the con-
sistently descending growth habit of R. tasmanica which virtually never grows upwards on
vertical substrates as R. physoloba does, 3) the lobule apex being obtuse rather than the
ac ute to acuminate as in R. physoloba 4) the complete absence ofmicrophyllous branching,
and 5) the 2- 5 small rather than one large oil body per lobe cell.
      Radula tasmanica can be distinguished from Radu/a tabu/aris by the lobule morphol-
ogy which in the latter has the carinal region narrowly inflated, and closely adpressed to the
lobe . When detached and flattened however, the lobules of both species appear very simi-
lar. Castle utilised perianth morphology to distinguish the two species, which in R. tasman-
ica is "e longate, truncate-clavate, terete below and gradually increasing in diameter" and in
                            M. A. M. RE NNER : New austral Radula records                            77


R. tabularis "oblong, increasing abruptly from a short terete base" (Castle 1963, p. 4).
     NOTES: Radula tasmanica has 'traditionally' been confused with R. physoloba. In
her paper on Radula, Hodgson (1944) quotes directly from Castle (1937) regarding how to
avoid confusing these two species " ... the cladogynous habit as well as other important di-
agnostic characters [of R. physoloba] have been overlooked with the result that the concep-
tion of the species has been extended in general to include two other forms which occur
within the same limits of distribution . One of these is R. tasmanica, which was described in
the Species Hepaticarum by Stephani, in 1909. This species is always smaller and the cari-
nal portion of the leaves is only narrowly inflated. It is, moreover, acrogynous and must be
totally unrelated to the Montage species". Castle identifies the inflated keel, imbricate
leaves with strongly concave lobes, and broadly inflated carinal region and the production
of microphyllous axes as characters definitive of R. physoloba. Ironically, Hodgson did not
take heed this advice, and of the specimens of R. tasmanica we have seen identified by her,
everyone is determined as R. physoloba.
      I have not examined the type of R. tabularis, and have relied on published accounts
and illustrations in Castle (1963), lones (1977) and Yamada (1979), and South African
specimens identified by lones for my concept of this species. We have examined a portion
of the type specimen of R. tasmanica, consisting of a short stem fragment, which possesses
the inflated carinal region illustrated by Castle, and is useful for differentiating this species
from R. tabularis. While I recognise that my knowledge of R. tabularis is incomplete, I
have examined material from New Zealand and Campbell Island identified by Castle as R.
tabularis, and found in all cases the specimens agree with R. tasmanica. I have also exam-
ined material upon which early published records of this species for New Zealand were
based, and found these referable to R. ratkowskiana. I have not encountered any material,

neither in the field, nor in the herbarium, which I am satisfied is R. tabularis, and accord-

RADULA_SOUTH_PACIFIC_2006  (1) 
J Hal/ori Bot. Lab. No. 99: 207-232 (Jail. 2006)




                    RADULA (RADULACEAE, MARCHANTIOPHYTA)
                              IN THE SOUTH PACIFIC

                                                                     M. L. SOl

ABSTRACT. A revision of Radula in the South Pacific is presented. Twenty four species are recog-
nized: R. aeufa Mitt.. R. amenfulosa Mitt. , R. deeurrens Mitt.. R.farmeri Pearson, R.fissijolia Steph.,
R.formosa (Meissn. ex Spreng.) Nees, R. iwatsukiana K.Yamada, R.javaniea Gottsche, R. kifagawae
K.Yamada, R. laeerafa Steph., R. longispiea Steph., R. nigra Pearson, R. l1ovoealedoniea Hurl. &
K.Yamada, R. novoealedoniensis K.Yamada, R. nymanii Steph., R. parvifexfa Steph., R. reflexa Nees
& Mont., R. rhombi/oba Steph., R. retroflexa Taylor, R. rupieola K. Yamada. R. seariosa Mitt., R. tji-
bodensis Goebel, R. ventrieosa Steph., and R. vieillardii Steph. Photomicrographs of the ventral view
of the plants are given for all species in this region. A key to the species in the region is provided.

KEY WORDS:             Radula, South Pacific, new synonyms

                                                                   INTRODUCTION

      This is a sequel to previous papers dealing with the genus Radula in New Zealand and
Tasmania (So 2005a) and Hawaii (So 2005b). Radula is a genus with 400 named species
listed in the Index Hepaticarum (Geissler & Bischler 1990). The centres of diversity are the
Paleotropics, Neotropics and Oceania, and a total of 150 species are recognized (Yamada
1986). The two earliest records in the Pacific region, R. rejiexa Nees & Mont. and R. javan-
ica Gottsche, date back to 1843 and 1845 respectively. A total of fifty two named species
have since been recorded from this region. This study, based on some recent collections,
recognized 24 species and 15 new synonyms are proposed.
