      Among the several species with apiculate apices of the leaf lobes described from the
South Pacific, R. aeuta is the earlist one . Three collections of the type material of R. aeuta
are present in Mitten's herbarium (NY), clearly portions of the main collection from
herbarium Ward. The plants are sparingly branched leaves contiguous to remote, branch
leaves similar to those on the main shoot in size. Marginal teeth occur sporadically, may be
totally absent in some leaves and sparsely so in others (fig. I a). This species was regarded
as conspecific with R. aneeps by Stephani (1884), probably based on the presence of scanty
marginal teeth around the leaf apex . There is a single perianth present, but unfortunately,
the mouth is battered providing no information on whether teeth are present or not. On the
other hand R. aneeps which is widespread in various parts of SE Asia and New Guinea, is
profusely pinnately branched and almost bushy in appearance (fig. I f), with all its leaves
distinctly dentate and the branches are considerably smaller in size; its cylindric perianths
armed with short teeth . Radu/a aeuta is very different from R. aneeps and here it is re-in-
stated as a separate species.
      Radula mueronata is the most obscure of all, known only from the type material, the
type locality still a mystery. This species was collected by T. Nightingale, who also gath-
ered the type material of R. eordiloba and R. retroflexa, all reportedly from the Pacific
Isles. As shown here in fig. I d, the material consists of only a short shoot of 2 mm long.
The leaf apex is apiculate, sometimes more or less subacute. An examination of the type
material of R. apieulata, R. el'enulata and R. paucidens as well as collections identified as
the better known R. apieulata shows that they are all have similar cell size and pattern, leaf
lobes and lobules. According to Castle ( 1961), all these species (except R. paueidens) are
known only in the sterile condition. However, among the large collections in VU, an old
collection of R. mueronata from Tahiti legit Nadeaud (YU) has a single perianth (shown
here in fig. le), with crenulate mouth, similar to that in R. paueidens . Here, R. apieuiata, R.
erenulata, R. mucronata, and R. paueidens and are placed under the synonymy of R. aeuta.

      Radu/a caledonica was, at different times, placed under the synonymy of R. amenlu-
/osa or R. formosa. The obovate lobules are similar to those of R. amentu/osa, different
from those of R. spicata or R. /ormosa. However, collections from New Caledonia vary
considerably in the shape of the lobules along the sectors of the shoot, most of them resem-
bling R. amentu/osa and some resembling R. /iJrl11()sa. Here, R. caledonica (fig. 2c) is re-
garded as conspecific with R. al11entu /osa, as already suggested by Kitagawa (1973). Radu-
/a recurvilohu/a, resembles R. amentu/osa in all its features, including leaf lobes, lobules,
cell pattern, large nodulose trigones and perianths. According the Yamada (1985), the most
important character of this species is the obovate to widely obovate leaf lobul es with
strongly recurved margins. However, as fig. 2d shows, the leaf margins are not particularly
recurved and most leaf lobes are similar to those in R. amenlu/osa. The lobules are quite
variable in size, large in some sectors and smaller in other sectors. similar to those typical
of R. amenlu/osa.
      Radu/a koghiensis was described as closely related to R. amentu/osa by Yamada
( 1985) and the base of the oblong-obovate leaf lobul es covers 1/3- 112 of the stem width
and leaf cells sli ghtly sma ll er and amentula numerous. But R. amentu/osa is fairly variable
in all of these features , espec ially the size of lobules, as shown here in fig. 2b. The lobul es
vary from subrectangulate to obovate, occupying 114- 3/4 the size of leaf lobes. Further-
more , the number of amentulose branches in severa l shoots present in the type of R.
koghiensis is similar to that in many collections of R. amenlu/osa from Fiji, its type locality
as well as those from the Solomon Islands and New Ca ledonica.

      In the illustration by Castle (1965: fig . 23), features pertaining to R. decurrens are the
distinctly reflexed apex of the lobules and the pronounced nodulose trigones of the leaf
cells. Castle (1965) in his comments on R. decurrens compared it with R. rejiexa, stating
that "This species, however, differs from the Nees & Montagne plant [R. reflexa] in that the
stem is broader, the lobe of the vegetative leaves more narrowly ovate and the perianth is
contracted at the mouth ." There is plenty of material in the type of R. decurrens in NY, and
the stems are more robust than those in R. reflexa. The apex of the lobules of many plants
in the holotype is not reflexed, a condition similar to the type of R. rejiexa. Furthermore,
leaf cells have no trigones at all. The ventral margin of some of the the leaf lobes in the
main shoot may sometimes be somewhat undulating, such condition also drawn in
Stephani's Icon. n. 8542. Many collections annotated and cited by Castle (1965) as R. de-
currens were originally identified as R. rejiexa. There is some hesitation to retain R. decur-
rens as a distinct species, perhaps a robust form of R. reflexa.
      At least four collection numbers of R. recurviloba are mentioned in the protologue,
but it is not clear why Castle (1965) listed Rechinger H4 as the lectotype, which, unfortu-
nately, was not included in the protologue, because only scanty material was present.

     Radula farmeri was previously treated by Castle (1961) as synonymous with R. lacer-

(1978: 30 \) who reinstated it as a distinct species. Yamada (1985 , \986) also regarded R.

gemmae of varying size (fig. 3b). Since marginal gemmae are rarely found in non-epiphyl-

      Radulafissi{olia resembles R. lacerala in having dentate margins around the incurved
apex of the leaf lobes. But unlike the latter, leaf cells have large trigones, and teeth around
the perianth are bluntly dissected, not laciniate. Radula fissifh/ia seems to be rare and in ad-
dition to the type, only one further collection was made over 50 years ago. Collections de-
scribed by Stephani as new species usually have "n. sp." or "sp. no." inscribed on the speci-
men label, many of them in the herbarium of General Paris, or indicated on the Icones. In
Stephani's herbarium (now located in G), there is only one collection of Radula fissifolia
"n. sp." Hence there is no question of selecting a lectotype as stated in Castle (1961). The
number 389 only refers to the herbarium number of General Paris and not the collection
number and isotypes deposited in other herbaria do not have "389" on them. The same is
true of several of Stephani's species treated here, e.g., R. fridens, R. longispica, and R. sim-
ilis.

     Radulu /ormosa is a hi g hl y va riable species with a me ntul a which do not occ ur a t the
base of every lobul e, a nd m ay be totally absent in some shoots . Leaf margin s may be
spreading or incurved as in the types of R. scariosa, R. novae-guineae and R. pycno/ejeu-
neoides. The leaf lobes m ay be co ntiguous to close ly imbricate and leaf lobules vary from
small subquadrate to subrectang ul a te, as in R. scariosa a nd R. novae-guineae, or obovate,
resem bling those in R. amentulosa. The lobule in the type material of R. spicuta is some-
what triangulate, comparatively la rger (shown here in fig. 3d) than those in collections from
 SE As ia , and so m e co llec ti ons have distinctly recta ng ul ate lobul es. The illustration of R.
forl11osa from Japa n in Yamada (1996, fig . 10) as we ll as Stepha ni 's (1985) Icon. n . 8858 on
 R. pycnolejeuneoides show the vari a bility in the lobules, from so mewhat triangulate to di s-
 tinctly subrectangulate, bases completely fused to the stem to partially covering it. Yamada
 (1999) and Castle (1950) had different opinions about the iden tity of all these species with
 a mentula . According to Yamada (1999), the base of leaf lobul e fully covers the stem in R.
./hrl11osa, and that in R. scariosa usually not. Howeve r, thi s feature is fairly variable. In th e
type material of R. novae-g uineae and R. spica fa and numerous collections of R . ./orl11osa
fro m Java, the ste m s a re fully ex posed, let alone hav ing the bases of lobule fully covering
the ste m.

Nadeaud 179, mixed with R. retroflexa (PC-0031641); 1855, Vieillard & Panch er 3 (PC, VU). VANU-
ATU. Emae Island, Maunga Repu Mountain, 25 Sep 1985, H. A. Miller /9522 (MU); Erromango,
Ipota, 800 ft, 26 Jun 1984, H. A. Miller 14685 (MU); Espirito Santo, Mt Malel, 30 km NNW of Lu-

       Numerous collections gathered from the South Pacific were initially identified as R.
javanica, one of the oldest species which is highly branched with oblong-ovate leaf lobes
and somewhat subquadrate lobules. Since most of the Radula species reported from this re-
gion are quite small, each having some distinctive features, they could easily be identified.
 Such small species include R. acuta, R. farmeri, R..fissifolia, R. lacerata, and R. vieillardii.
 But R. javanica seems to be a 'reservoir name' for plants which are larger, highly branched
and with a clavate-truncate perianth. Radula multifiora was the first species segregated
from the large pool of specimens annotated as "R. javanica." According to Castle (1966),
 R. multifiora differs from R. javanica in having more imbricate leaf lobes and perianths
with a contracted mouth . The type material in FH has several perianths, three of them with
slightly contracted mouths (but the same is true in the type of R. javanica, fig . 4c), and
some without a contracted mouth . Castle (1965) then described three more species (R. un-
dulifiora , R. oceanica and R. densifolia) all based on collections previously identified as R.
javanica. The lobules are quite variable along the sectors of these large plants. The apices
of many of the lobules are slightly turned towards the margin, especially in the older sec-
tors of the shoot. An examination of all of Castle's collections in YU shows that these are
variations of R. javanica, irregularly pinnately branched, with imbricate leaves and peri-
anths similar to that of R. javanica . Radula pinnulata, an obscure species from New Cale-
donia, is known only from the type and there has not been further collections even from the
type locality. Castle (1965) had a detailed description of the species and the apex of the
 lobule was "broadly rounded often with a plicate in the outer lateral margin." However,
other sectors of the plants show that the apices are sometimes turned towards the margin
(fig. 40. Radula similis is known almost only from the type and the small plants in the type
material were found on bark, resembling R. javanica.

     Radula kitagawae, a rare sl?ecies known only from the type, resembles a small form of
R. vieillardii, with smaller leaf lobes, 0.6- 0.7 mm long, and cells in the leaf lobes are also
large, averaging 36 pm in size, with papulose walls. The cells have thick cuticle and very
large trigones.

      Radula lacerata, type from New Caledonia, is easily confused with R. anceps due to
their si mil ar habit, morphological features of the leaf lobes, lobul es, cell pattern and size of
perianths. Until now, the only appreciable difference between the two species is the peri-
anth which has long laciniate teeth around the mouth in R. lace rata whereas teeth are small
and of similar size in R. anceps. However, in the type material of R. lacerata, most peri-
anths have long laciniate teeth around the mouth, but there are also perianths with short
blunt teeth, somewhat si milar to those in R. anceps. Numerous collections from New Cale-
donia were originally annotated as R. anceps and it is highly likely that these two species
are conspecific.

      Radula longispica is known almost only from the type and has been reported only
once since it was first described from New Caledonia (Hiirl iman n 1978). According to
Castle (1967), thi s is the only species in this region exhibiting caducity and shoots are al-
most denuded, leav ing only persistent lobules along the wiry branches, and the androecia
are long spicate, with 10- 30 bracts. Marginal gemmae occur on some caducous leaf lobes
(fig. 5b insert). The loss of leaf lobes may also have been caused by environmental facto rs
since the plants in many collections from Vanuata have both intact leaf lobes as well as
shoots with caducous lobes. The plants resemble R. reflexa in many of their features, espe-
cially the long spicate androecia, but the apex of leaf lobules is never reftexed. Two coll ec-
222                                  J Hattori Bot. Lab. No. 99                            2 0 0 6


tions originall y identified and reported as R. campanigera by (Campbe ll 1971) from Ker-
madec Islands were dcscribed as a new subspecies of R. cordifoba by Renner (2005). How-
ever, all the features agree with that of R. longispica.

      Radufa nigra is an obscure species and not available to Castle (1967) when he was
monographing thi s genus. Neither is there any other collection in all herbaria. The type ma-
terial in BM has plenty of very sooty plants, hence the species epithet. The leaves are wide-
ly spreading, with large subquadrate lobules and cells with nodulose trigones . Perhaps en-
vironmental factors were responsible for the very dark colour when the plants were dried.
A number of collcction s from New Caledonia by Guillaum in & Baumann were all annotat-
ed and publishcd as R. rohinsonii Steph. (type from Australia, Norfolk Island, I. Rohinson
s.n., F. Muller mi sit, herb. Stephani , ex herb. Melbourne, holotype: G-22377!; isotype:
FH I) by Hurlimann (1978), but all features, including the lobules, are similar to those of R.
nigra. Plants in the type material of R. nigra are sterile, but those by Guillaumin & Bau-
mann have male and female organs (fig. 5d). Male plants slightly smaller than female ones,
androecia terminal, intercalary, or on lateral branches, bracts in 4-5 pairs; gynoecia termi -
nal on main shoot, with two innovations, repeatedly fertile; perianths clavate, 3.2- 3.5 mm
long, mouth 1.0- 1.3 mm wide, entire; sporophyte not seen. Gemmae lacking.

    Description: Hlirlimann & Yamada (1979: 238).

      Radula l1ovocaledonica , a rare endemic of New Caledonia (Yamada 1985) and known
almost only from the type, is the only paroicous species in this region. Since paroicous
species are of rare occurrence in Radufa, R. novoca fedonica species could not be confused
with the two paroicous species in Oceania and the South Pacific: R. sainsburiana E.A.
Hodgs. & Allison from New Zealand and Tasmania and R. hawaiica M.L. So, endemic to
Hawaii (So 2005b). Compared with these two species, R. novocaledonica is the smallest,
not more than 4 mm long with gynoecia on several short lateral branches and 3-4 male
brac ts below.

      Specimens examined. NEW CALEDONIA. Mont Panie, 800-1200 m, on fallen log, 29 Jul 1982,

     Radula novocaledoniensis is rather rare and only two additional collections are
known. Marginal gemmae are of rare occurrence and are seen in only two leaves. This
species resembles R. nigra, also endemic to New Caledonia, in all its features, but the latter
species has a slightly larger and more quadrate lobule, and the apex of lobules in R. novo-
caledoniensis is more acute.

15. Radula nymanii Steph., Sp. Hepat. 6 : 516. 1924.                                    (Fig. 6a)
- Type: Papua New Guinea. Morobe, Sattelberg, Oct 1899, £. Nyman s.n. (holotype : G-18117! ; iso-
     types: S-B43476 1, S-B43477!, S-B43478!, S-B43479!).
     Illustrations: Castle (1939 : fig. 7); Stephani (1985: Icon. n. 8833-4).
     Description: Castle (1939: 39).
     Specimens examined. NEW CALEDONIA. Me Ori , N of Moindou, 550 m, on leaves, 9 Aug 1982,
N. Kitagawa 22673 (NICH); lie des Pins, 10 m, epiphyllous, 13 Aug 1982, N. Kitaga wa s.n. (Iwatsu-
ki & Mizutani : Bryoph. Exs. Fasc. 4, no. 197) (BISH, M, S-B92700).

     Radula nymanii is rather rare in this region and only two collections are known . It dif-
fers from R. tjibodensis, the only other truly epiphyllous species in having an elongated
apex of the lobules. Radula nymanii resembles R. protensa Lindenb. in Meissn. , a more
common species in SE Asia, and according to Castle (1939: 41), the apical portion of the
leaf lobule in R. protensa is somewhat longer and broader than that in R. nymanii and is
turned abruptly outward. But as can be seen in the illustrations of R. nymanii in Castle
(1939 : fig. 7), the apex of leaf 10buIes varies considerably in size and the abruptness of
turning outward. Radula protensa is previously distinguished from R. nymanii by produc-
ing gemmae only on the ventral surface of the leaf lobes, whereas gemmae in R. nymanii
are marginal on the leaf. However, gemmae in the holotype of R. protensa in W occur at
the margin as well as on the ventral leaf surface. Radula nymanii could probably be a form
of R. protensa.

     Radula parvitexta is highly branched and has subquadrate lobules with large trigones
in the cells. This is rare in the South Pacific and only one collection is known . [t resembles
R. robinsonii and these two species may probably be conspecific .

     Radu/a rupico /a, only male plants known , resembles R. vieillardii in its cell pattern

21. Radula scariosa Mitt. , Bonplandia 9: 367.1861. Sp. Hepat. 4: 234 . 1910. (Fig.7b)
- Type: Fiji Island. Viti Island Seell1ann inter 837 (holotype: NY!).
R. ha/ansae Steph., Sp. Hepat. 4: 208. 1910; syn. novo - Type: New Caledonia. B. Ba/ansa s.n. (holo-
     type: G-19562').
     Illustrations: Castle (1950: fig. 5 as R. scariosa; 1967: fig. 42 as R. ba/ansae); Stephani (1985:
Icon. n. 8718 as R. ha/ansae. n. 8857 as R. scariosa); Yamada (1985: fig. 8).
     Descriptions: Castle (1950: 270 as R. scariosa; 1967: 85 as R. ha/ansae); Yamada (1985: 126).

      Radu/a scariosa is an obscure species and features pertaining to this species include
the falcate leaf lobes and lobules fused with the stem with no free margin. But as discussed
under R. /c)rmosae, specimens gathered from various regions in the Pacific show immense
variations in the degree to which the lobules are fused to the stem or the extent to which
their bases cover the stem. It is puzzling to notice that the type material of R. spicata, R.
scariosa and R. amentu/osa, together with some shoots of R. formosa all came from the
same collection "Seem ann inter 837" in Fiji . In fact, this collection also contains shoots of
R. ref/exa and R. retrof/exa. The type material of R. sCQI-iosa contains scanty material of
two short shoots with a perianth (fig. 7b), and that of R. amentu/osa also contains scanty
material of a few shoots. But the type material of R. spicata in NY has plenty of material
and it seems this packet is the original collection from which material of all three species:
R. amentu/osa, R. scariosa and lastly, R. spicata, were described. An examination of all the
shoots in this large collection of R. spicata reveals that the lobule is quite variable along the
length of the shoot which may reach 5 cm or more. Perianths always have two innovations,
                                 M. L. So : Rat/uta in the South Pac ific                           227




         Fig. 7. a. Radula rupicola K.Yamada (from holotype, NICH); b. Radula scariosa
   Mitt. (from holotype, NY); c. Radula tjibodensis Gottsche (from holotype of R. rein eckeana ,
   G) ; d, e. Radula venfricosa Steph . (d from Robinson s.n. as R. mittenii, FH; e from holotype
   of R. subpallens, G) ; f. Radula vieillardii Gottsche ex Steph. (from holotype, G). All X 14.



repeatedly fertile . Some lobules in the shoots are wholly fused to the stem whereas those in
other sectors of the shoots cover part of the stem, and some are intermediate between these
two conditions. Radula novae-guineae whose lobul e is fully fused with the stem has al-
ready been placed under the synonymy of R. formosa by Hi.irlimann (1978) and variations
of the lobes and lobules are fully depicted in his illustrations. A collection of R.formosa by
228                                         J. Hattori Bot. Lab. No. 99                                     2 0 0 6


van Zanfen 68-2664/a from the Solomon Islands have three different forms, one simil ar to
R. spicafa, one similar to R. scariusa, but the base of lobul es sometimes slightly covers the
stem, and one with rectangul ate lobules which do not cover the stem at all. It is not unusual
to find at least two different form s (sometimes three) in the same co llecti on from the
Solomon Islands. Whether all these speci es with amentula are variations of the same
spec ies awaits future mo lec ul ar analysis. Here R. scariosa is hes itantl y retain ed as a di s-
tinct species.
      Radu/a ba/ansae is known only from the type whi ch consists of only a short sector of
shoot with amentula and very small leaves, the amentul a were not drawn in Castle ( 1967:
fig. 42). Furthermore, cell s have large nodulose tri gones, all indi cations that the pl ant prob-
ably belongs to R. scariosa.

(1975: 74 as R. /oriana, 81 as R. subpallens, 83 as R. venlricosa).
     Specimens examined. AUSTRALIA. Norfolk Island, Robinson s. n. as R. miflenii (NY) . INDONESIA.
Java, Tjiburrum, 1900 m, H. Surge/f 1829p.p. as R. subpallens (lE); Java, Toegoe, 1150 m, 1861 , W
S. Kurz 793p.p. as R. venfricosa (lE). SOLOMON ISLA NDS. Mt Popemanaseu, 2000- 2200 m, 1968, van
Zanlen 68-2602/c as R. venlricosa (JE); Guadalcanal , Mt Popemanaseu, 1500- 1600 m, 1968, van
Zanlen 611-25691d I , 68-2568b as R. subpallens (lE, L); Gaudalcanal , Mt Gallego, 3200 ft, 16 Oct
1966, G. F C. Dennis 20025a as R. /oriana (JE, S).
     Radula ventricosa has imbricate leaf lobes with a subquadrate lobule having a plica
sometimes covering 3/4 the stem or even extending across it. Radula subpallens is similar
to R. ventricosa, both having similar cell size, cell pattern and trumpet-shaped perianths.
But the lobules in collections of R. ventricosa are quite variable in the extent to which the
stem is covered, varying from 1/2 to extending across the stem . Leaf lobules in R. ventri-
cosa are not always imbricate, but similar to R. subpallens (Yamada 1975 : figs . 15, 16).
Furthermore, collections identified as R. subpallens show the same variations in all of the
above features. In fact , part of the type material of R. subpallens legit W. S. Kurz 793 from
Java was also identified as R. ventricosa.
     Radula loriana was regarded as a new species by Castle (1959: 6) based on a speci-
men from New Guinea determined by Stephani as R. javanica. Thi s species may be a weak
form of R. ventricosa . From the range of specimens annotated as R. ventricosa, R. subpal-
lens and R. loriana, the lobules vary considerably in the degree of plication as well as the
extent to which the stem is "covered' by the apical portion. Since R. loriana is known only
in the sterile condtion, it is here tentatively placed under the synonymy of R. ventricosa,
which differs from all species in Oceania in it dichotomous branching.

      Even though R. vieillardii is a rare species, it is easily separated from all other species
in this region by its lace-like cell pattern and fan-like leaf lobes which are widely spread-
ing. Moreover, the leaf cells are the largest of all species, sometimes up to 45 pm.

RADULA_SYNOPSIS_NZ_TAS_2005  (1) 
J Hattori Bot. Lab. No. 98: 149- /74 (Aug. 2005)




  A SYNOPSIS OF RADULA (RADULACEAE, MARCHANTIOPHYTA)
              IN NEW ZEALAND AND TASMANIA

                                                   M. L. SOl

ABSTRACT. A total of 36 species names of Radula have been recorded from New Zealand and Tas-
mania. Of this number only 15 are recognized: Radula acutiloba Steph., R. aneurismalis (Hook.f. &
Taylor) Gottsche, Lindenb. & Nees, R. australiana K. Yamada, R. buccinifiera (Hook.f. & Taylor)
Gottsche, Lindenb. & Nees, R. dentifolia Grolle, R. grandis Steph., R. marginata Taylor ex Gottsche,
Lindenb. & Nees, R. novae-hollandiae Hampe ex Lehm., R. physoloba Mont., R. plicata Mitt., R.
retrojl.exa Taylor, R. sainsburiana E. A. Hodgs., R. scariosa Mitt., R. tasmanica Steph., and R. uvifera
(Hook.f. & Taylor) Gottsche, Lindenb. & Nees. Seven new synonyms are proposed. Radula cavifolia
Hampe ex Gottsche, Lindenb. & Nees, R. madagascariensis Mont., R. tabularis Steph., and R. cae-
spitosa Steph., are excluded from this region.
KEY WORDS: Radula, New Zealand and Tasmania, new synonyms

                                               INTRODUCTION
      Radula is a genus with 400 named species listed in the Index Hepaticarum (Geissler
& Bischler 1990). The centres of diversity are the Paleotropics, Neotropics and Oceania,
and a total of 150 species are recognized (Yamada 1986). Renner & Braggins (2004) recog-
nized 28 species in this region. The two earliest records, R. novae-hollandiae Hampe ex
Lehm. and R . physoloba Mont., date back to 1838 and 1844 respectively. This genus had
been divided in three sub genera and 11 sections by Castle (1937,1950,1959,1962,1965,
1967) who, in his-life long study had treated all sections extensively and globally. Different
species of Radula are classified according to a number of features, including sexuality,
presence and position of gemmae, shape of leaf lobes and lobules, especially the attach-
ment of the lobules to the stem, presence of large trigones, and shape of perianths etc.
However, these features are quite variable and they may differ considerably from the lower
sectors to those near the apex. In some species e.g. R. buccinifera and R. plicata, the lobule
is quite small compared to the size of leaf lobes, and this feature is fairly constant. Howev-
er, in R. retrofiexa, the lobules may be almost parallel to the stem in some sectors, but dis-
tinctly directed towards the margin in other sectors. Leaf cells may be papulose or with a
papilla in a number of species, notably R . tasmanica and R. physoloba, but the occurrence
of papillae in different collections is rather sporadic, may be more distinct in some but
barely so in others.
      Specimens examined in the present study include recent collections from Tasmania
and New Zealand. Of the 36 species names recorded from this region, only 15 are accepted
here. Seven new synonyms are proposed. Radula cavifolia from Java, R. madagascariensis,
R . tabularis and R. caespitosa, all from Africa, are excluded from this region. Due to the
small number of species recognized, the 11 sections created by Castle (1965) are not adopt-

   I   Biology Department, Hong Kong Baptist University, 224 Waterloo Road, Hong Kong.
150                                                   1. Hattori Bot. Lab. No. 98                                                     200 5


ed here. Photomicrographs of the ventral view of each recognized species are included.
Since detailed descriptions of each species are given in Hodgson (1944) and Castle (1937,
1962, 1965, 1967), they will not be repeated here.

      The presence of numerous gemmae fringing the leaf margin and scattering on the ven-
tral surface of the leaf lobes easily separates R. acutiloba from all other species of Radula
in this region . Another feature is the pinnate branching pattern, rarely seen in other species.
Radula papulosa was synonymized by Yamada (1987: 195) with R. acutiloba, common in
Australia, based evidently on the frequent occurrence of gemmae. Stephani's Icon. n. 8668
of R. papulosa was drawn from Colenso a1691, a collection lacking in G and Castle (1967)
had chosen Colenso a1643 which lacks the inscription "n. sp." as lectotype. It is not clear
why Castle had not selected Colenso a 1450 which bears the inscription "n. sp." as the lec-
totype. However, a search through various herbaria shows that Colenso a1691 is present in
WELT.

     Radula aneurismalis is a puzzling little known species and there is some confusion
154                                 1. Hattori Bot. Lab. No. 98                       2 005


about its exact identity. When Hooker & Taylor (1845) described it, both male and female
plants were mentioned. However, Stephani's Icon n. 8755 only showed the male plants
which consist of two slender shoots with globular leaves, resembling those of R. cavifolia.
Radula cavi{olia was synonymized by Castle (1963) with R. aneurismalis, based perhaps
on similarities of the globular leaf lobes in the male plants, but Yamada (1984) doubted
such placement. There was no indication that Castle (\963), while listing numerous speci-
mens of R. cav!{olia from various regions of SE Asia, had seen the female plants of R.
aneurismalis. The figure of R. aneurismalis in Castle (1963: fig. 12) is a composite of both
species, from the male plant of R. aneurismalis and the female plant of R. cavifolia. An ex-
amination of the type material of both species confirmed Yamada's opinion (Fig. Ic, 8b).
Radula cavifolia, common in various parts of Asia, is among the smallest in the genus,
measuring not more than \ mm wide with leaves (usually 0.6- 0.8 mm). The leaves are al-
most globular and the lobules almost half the size of the lobes. The perianths (Fig. 8b) have
a long and narrow base and quite different from that in R. aneurismalis (Fig. Id, f).
      The type material in FH only consists of male plants about 1.2 mm wide with leaves
(shown here in Fig. Ic). As commented by Hooker & Taylor (1845), the perianth is large
compared to the size of the plants. The leaves are closely imbricate with broad lobules con-
tinuous with the dorsal margin of the leaf lobe. Castle (1963) described Radula compacta
as a new species and the material listed under R. compacta were all previously identified by
Rodway as R. aneurismalis (Fig. I f).
      An examination of the large collections of Radula located in HO shows that some col-
lections identified as R. aneurismalis belong to R. tasmanica and those labelled as R.
physoloba actually belong to R. aneurismalis . As drawn by Castle (1963) and Stephani
(1985), it was originally thought that R. aneurismalis is a very tiny species, resembling
members of Lejeuneaceae. A detailed examination of all these collections reveals some-
thing quite startling. Only two collections consist of smal1leaves and amentula with globu-
lar leaves, typical of the male plants of R. aneurismalis. Most collections have larger leaves
similar to the type of R. compacta (plants 1.5 mm wide with leaves), bearing normal
branches and leaves, while some collections have normal shoots bearing both normal
branches and very small amentula with globular leaves (Fig. I f). Some collections (notably
HO-305593, HO-672l9) lack the normal branches altogether but are composed almost en-
tirely of amentula-like branches (0.5 mm wide with leaves) bearing only globular leaves,
apparently in a perpetual vegetative state, giving the plants a powdery appearance in the
dry condition (Fig. 2c). Some light can be found in Rodway's (1916) comment about R.
aneurismalis: "I have specimens in which the ends of the shoots of typical R. aneurysmalis
have grown into typical forms of R. tasmanica .. ." The mystery of the apparent polymorphic
nature of this species can now be solved. Figure le here probably shows what Rodway had
seen where tiny shoots of R. aneurismalis grow into larger normal shoots typical of "R.
compacta." A few collections even have amentula bearing clusters of very small crowded
leaves (Fig. 2d), apparently subjected to the same conditions found in R. colensoi, to be
discussed under R. physoloba .

    Radula australiana, type from New South Wales and previously only male plants
known, seems to have a wider distribution. Numerous collections from various parts of
New Zealand were reported by Renner (2005) and the perianths resemble that of R. tas-
manica, with a narrow base.

80- 250m, Schii/er-Verwimp & Verwimp 13646/A, 12 Jan 1991 (herb. Schiifer-Verwimp); Hawkes
Bay, Matamau, dripping banks in forest, W. N. Beckett s.n. as R. mittenii, 24 Apr 1899, herb. Levier
1905 (FH, STR, VU); Great Barrier Island, T. Kirk 143 (G, VU); South Auckland, Pirongia forest
Park, Waite Road Track, 390m, M. A. M. Renner & D. J. Clarke 01/99 as R. si/vosa, 29 Apr 2001
(WELT); Auckland Island, ridge S of Lake Speight, 360 m, P N. Johnson 20/3, 20 Feb 1973 (WELT).
      Radula buccinifera is the most common species of Radula in this region . The peri-
anths usually have a narrow base, but the base may sometimes be as wide as the mouth
even in the same collection. Compared with all other species, the plants can easily be iden-
tified by the small, almost quadrate lobules attached to the stem for most of the length. The
leaf lobes may sometimes be fa\cate, and the leaf apex rounded to slightly acute, resem-
bling R. plicata, but the perianths of the latter species have plicae which are lacking in R.
buccinifera . As can be seen from the lengthy list of synonyms, R. buccinifera has been de-
scribed under several different names, indicating the variability exhibited by the various
forms. As already discussed by Hodgson (1944: 286), R. silvasa is nearest to R. levieri, for
which it is difficult to distinguish . However, the differences such as "more crowded leaves,"
and "dorsal lobes overlapping the stem," and the "whole plant is stouter and more com-
pact," do not justify the status of a separate species. Moreover, the perianths being "stouter
without the elongated tubular neck of R. levieri" are also seen in many collections of R.
buccinifera. AlIision & Child (1975) already commented that "R. silvasa is rather a variety
of R. buccinifera." Radula parviretis, known only from the type material in the sterile con-
dition, was described as having peculiar cell types. But an examination of the type material
shows that this species is conspecific with R. buccinifera.
      The type material of R. wattsiana only contains sterile material and this species bears
superficial resemblance to R. buccinifera in the leaf lobes, lobules and size of cells lacking
trigones. Rodway (1916: 81) commented that this species is very like R. buccinifera, but
the lobule is more inflated and concave. Collections of R. wattsiana with perianths, identi-
fied by Stephani (in HO and G), all belong to R. buccinifera.

      Radula dentifolia is characterized by having several marginal teeth around the leaf
apex. However, the presence of teeth may be sporadic. They are usually more numerous in
young leaves close to the apical sector of the stem, whereas mature leaves may be devoid of
teeth, and the very acute leaf apex is often cuspidate (Fig. 3c). Another feature of this
species is the very long tubular perianth (8- 10 mm) with a crenulate mouth, but perianths
also may be as short as 3 mm. Perianths of all other species in this region never reach 6 mm
(usually 3- 5 mm). Hodgson (1944 : 284) already speculated that R. cuspidata is "only a
form of R. dentata." As can be seen from Fig. 3c, d, both species are strikingly similar and
R. cuspidata is here synonymized with R. dentifolia. In fact, several collections gathered by
T. Kirk from Great Barrier Island, the type locality of R. cuspidata, were identified as R.
dentata and/or R. cuspidata .

other and completely covering the stem, is unique among all species of Radula in this re-

ginal gemmae are seen only in two collections. Until now, the perianth is still unknown.
However, an old collection by Sinclair from New Zealand (Fig . 3f) has several perianths
arising from the main shoot, with two subftoral innovations ; perianths 4- 5 mm long, 1.75-
1.8 mm wide, mouth wide, wing lacking, base slightly narrow; sporophyte not seen.

     Radula marginata is unique in the presence of a distinct thick leaf margin (Fig. 4b)
composed of 3-4 layers of smaller cells (12- 15 fJ.m) compared with the larger leaf cells
(20- 25 fJ.m), thus making the leaf lobes slightly concave when viewed ventrally. It could
probably be confused with R. retroflexa, both having similar oblong leaf lobules, parallel
with the stem. The lobules in the latter usually have the apex slightly turned towards the
leaf margin, but this feature is not well expressed in some sectors, thereby resembling those
of R. marginata (compare Hodgson 1944: fig 5 as R. helmsiana and fig. 9 as R. marginata).
However, R. retroflexa does not have the horny margin of smaller cells.

     Radula physoloba is the earliest species of Radula described from New Zealand. The
type material was collected from the Auckland Islands where that of R. uvifera was also
collected at about the same time. As already commented by Hodgson (1944), these two
species can easily be confused in the sterile condition. Both have similar size, branching
pattern, leaf lobes and lobules and even the presence of amentula of varying lengths. Peri-
anths are found on leading shoots as well as on lateral branches. The only clear distinguish-
ing feature is the perianth with a narrow neck and numerous warty ridges at the middle
portion in R. uvifera (Fig. 8a) whereas perianths in R. physoloba are smooth and do not
have a narrow base. According to Hodgson (1944), R. physoloba exhibits several forms of
162                                     J. Hattori Bot. Lab. No. 98                              200 5

microphylly, and Allison & Child (1975) also mentioned the "spikelets of crowded tiny
leaflets." In collections gathered by Colenso, besides the typical amentula of varying
lengths, there are also many branches formed by dense clusters of much smaller bilobed
leaves (Fig. 4 f) giving the plants an overall untidy and smudgy appearance. Castle (1937)
speculated that these might have been due to nematode infestation. This mystery is solved
by a microscopic examination indeed revealing the presence of nematodes in plants of R.
colensoi collected by Colenso no. a1318 (Fig. 4f insert). Perhaps Colenso collected from
certain sites infested with nematodes! Such clusters are also seen in recent collections of R .
physoloba from New Zealand and R. aneurismalis from Tasmania.
      Some confusion surrounds the identity of R. physoloba. Many collections from this
region in Castle's herbarium (located in VU), initially identified as R. physoloba and R. tas-
manica were labelled as R. tabularis (type from S. Africa) and cited as such by Castle
(1963 : 41) . Indeed, R. tabularis resembles R. physoloba in many of its features, especially
the lobules and habit (Fig. 8e). If these two species are conspecific, R. tabularis should be
placed under the synonymy of R. physoloba . The confusion between R. physoloba and R.
tasmanica could have arisen from the shape of the smaller branches of R. physoloba which,
by themselves, resemble those of R. tasmanica, and if only scanty material is available,
e.g., branches only, it is almost impossible to correctly identify whether it belongs to one or
the other.
      The occurrence of R. physoloba in Australia is doubtful. As discussed earlier, collec-
tions from Tasmania identified as R. physoloba are all referable to either R. tasmanica or R.
aneurismalis due to the confusion about the identity of R. aneurismalis.

     Radula retroflexa has a wide distribution, from Japan to New Zealand, and all regions
in between, extending further eastward to Hawaii . For over 150 years, this species was de-
scribed under several different names. It was first described as early as 1846 by Taylor from
a collection by T. Nightingale from the Pacific Isles as R. retroflexa which is characterized
by the subrhombic leaf lobules which are sometimes parallel to the stem with the apex di-
rected toward the leaf margin (Fig. 5b).
     Radula fauciloba from New Guinea (Fig. 5d) was regarded as a variety of R.
retroflexa by Yamada (1979). Collections from New Zealand and Australia in various
herbaria were mostly identified as R. fauciloba prior to 1979. Yamada (1996) eventually
synonymized this variety with R. retroflexa . But Castle (1965) already placed the New
164                                       1. Hattori Bot. Lab. No. 98                             200 5




            Fig. 5. a. Radula plicata Mitt. (from holotype, NY); b- d. Radula retrojiexa Taylor (b
       from holotype of R. retrof/exa, BM ; c from isotype of R. allisonii, MPN ; d from isotype of R.
      fauciloba Steph., VU); e, f. Radula sainsburiana E. A. Hodgs. & Allison (e from Berggren
      s.n., VU; f from Schllster, ScOII & Tavlor, MPN). All X 14.

Zealand species of R. helmsiana under the synonymy of R. fauciloba. It is probable that a
few more species of Radula described from Oceania could be con specific with R.
retrojlexa. Castle (1961) provided a detailed discussion on the error made by Hodgson
(1944) when describing and illustrating R. allisonii and R. helmsiana. As can be seen from
                      M. L. So: A synopsis of Ragula in New Zealand and Tasmania                 165


the evolution of the understanding of R. retroflexa (Yamada 1979, 1986, 1996), the status
of R. allisonii as a distinct species is no longer tenable. Some of the collections identified
as R. allisonii were initially labelled as R. helmsiana. An examination of the specimens of
R. allisonii shows that it is conspecific with R. retroflexa, both having narrowly and irregu-
larly winged perianths. Castle (1965) theorised that the lobules in R. allisonii are not turned
towards the leaf margin whereas those in R. helmsiana do deflect from the stem. However,
the illustrations by Yamada (1996: fig. 238, D) on the variability exhibited by different
plants or even different sectors of the plant of R. retroflexa show that the shape of the lob-
ule varies considerably, from almost straight, parallel with the stem, to distinctly directed
towards the margin.

     The small size and paroicy of R. sainsburiana make it a unique species in this region.
Even though most of the collections cited here are sterile, the small plant size, together
with ovate leaf lobes resembling those of Lejeuneaceae enable correct identification. The
magnification in the illustration by Castle (1963: fig. 19A) seems incorrect. The leaf lobes
should be about 0.6 mm long, but those illustrated exceed 1. 1 mm long and the branches
should be considerably smaller and slenderer. This species resembles a miniature R.
plicata, having similar leaf lobes and lobules. But R. plicata, being dioicous, differs in hav-
ing several plicae on the wall of the perianth.

2775 (lE).
      Microphyllous branches known as amentula are of infrequent occurrence in all the
species treated so far. But they are of regular occurrence in R. multiamentula. This species
was synonymized by Yamada (1999) with R. scariosa, a species described from Fiji, based
on their general similarities. The holotype of R. scariosa contains scanty material (4 short
shoots not more than 2 mm long and two perianths, shown here in Fig. 6c). The plants are
slightly larger than R. multiamentula and the leaf lobes and amentula are rather loosely im-
bricate and not as compact and neat as those in the latter. The male plants of R. multiamen-
tula was unknown hitherto, but one collection from Tasmania has two male shoots in the
position of amentula, bearing androecia, shortly spicate with 3-4 bracts (Fig. 6b, d).
      Hodgson (1962: 122) reiterated the reason for describing R. multiamentula from a col-
lection legit Hooker from Auckland Island that was mixed with the type material of R.
aquilegia Hook. f. & Taylor from Ireland (Ireland, Miss Hutchinson s.n., FH!). However,
there is no indication that she had seen this Hooker specimen (deposited in FH) which ac-
tually belongs to R. uvifera and not to R. multiamentula. Castle (1937 : 48) did not mention
the regular occurrence of amentulose branches, but only admitted that "this material is sim-
ilar to Montagne's [R. physoloba] species." Castle was apparently referring to R. uvifera
which he later described in detail in 1950. At the same time, Castle (1950: 260) also gave a
brief description of the "species collected by Weymouth and others from Tasmania," obvi-
ously referring to R. multiamentula which was later described by Hodgson (1962). It is not
clear why Castle (1968 : 10) did not include R. multiamentula in his Synopsis of the genus
under section 2 Amentulosae Steph.

     Radula tasmanica has very inflated leaf lobules and some plants resemble R. physolo-
ba. This species has in the past been confused with R. tabularis, an African species which
is quite different, not only in the shape of the leaves but also in the branches. Reports of R.
caespitosa by Hodgson (1965) are all referable to R. tasmanica. Weymouth collected ex-
tensively in various parts of Tasmania from 1899- 1914 and collections from Williamsford,
the type locality of R. tasmanica, all belong to this species. Many of these collections by
Weymouth were identified by Stephani (as written on the specimen labels) as R. physoloba.
Some of these were cited by Castle (1963: 38) as R. tasmanica, but some as R. tabularis
(Castle 1963: 41), and three collections, Levier n. 5523, n. 3659 and Weymouth 1453 were
cited under both species.
      Radula ratkowskiana resembles R. tasmanica in all its features, including the perianth
with a narrow base (Fig. 6f). According to the original description, R. ratkowskiana is char-
acterized by having a dorsal papilla over each lumen. However, this "papilla over each
lumen" is also found in the type material of R. tasmanica and even in some collections of
R. physoloba from Lord Auckland's Group, legit Hooker (Fig. 4e, insert). The occurrence
of these papillae is rather sporadic and those in the type material of R. ratkowskiana as well
as some collections from the type locality of R. tasmanica are rather well developed (Fig.
7a, b).

VU); North Island, Urewera Ecological Region, Waikaremoana Ecological District, Manuoha track,
on ridge leading to summit in vicinity of spot height 1017, 1000m, M. A. M. Renner 01146a, May
2001 (HO); Northland, Waipoua Kauri Forest, 66 km north of Dargavill, 320 m, Schiifer-Verwimp &

s.n. , Dec 1898 (M-0088267).

      The type materials of Radula uvifera and R. physoloba were collected from the same
locality and they have similar microphyllous branches and the leaf lobes with recurved
apices continuous with the lobule (Fig. 7d) . According to the key in Hodgson (1944 : 274),
the only difference between them is the regularly pinnate microphyllous branches in R. uv-
ifera whereas those "may appear in all stages from microphyllous to ordinary ones" in R.
physoloba. However, when Hodgson (1962: 123) described R. multiamentula, the amentula
in R. uvifera were described as "irregular in length and not so numerous or regular." It
seems the presence of several warty ridges at the middle portion of the perianth in R. uv-
ifera could be a more distinctive and reliable feature that separates the two species. Such
plicae are lacking in R. physoloba (Fig. 8a). Hodgson (1944 : 277) commented that in the
absence of perianths, R. uvifera can easily be confused with R. physoloba .
     One collection by Goebel from Arthur's Pass is intriguing in having a sterile shoot with amentu-
la having strongly incurved apices, similar to those described by Yamada (1987) as R. thiersiae K.
Kamada from Queensland (Yamada 1987: fig. 2). In fact, all three types of branches are seen in a sin-
gle collection: normal branches, typical amentula and hook-shaped ones (Fig. 7g).

                                              EXCLUDENDA

1.    Radula caespitosa Steph., Hedwigia 27: 107. 1888. - Type: Reunion, "Bourbon, Lep-
      ervanche s.n., herb. Bescherelle" (holotype :G-10609!; isotypes: FH!, G-27420!, G-
       27421!, YU!). (Fig. 8f)
      lIlustrations: Castle (1963: fig. 180, F as R. tabularis); Stephani (1985: Icon. n. 8672 as R. cae-
spitosa).
     Radula caespitosa was recorded by Hodgson (1965 : 86) from various parts of New Zealand, and
these collections (later labelled as R. tabularis) located in MPN all belong to R. tasmanica. Radula
caespitosa was synonymized by Castle (1963) with R. tabulan's, but was regarded as conspecific with
R. madagascariensis by Jones (1977). An examination of the type material of these three species
shows that R. caespitosa does not resemble either of the species (Fig. 8d- f). Radula madagascarien-
sis has very widely spreading leaf lobes which are so fragile and caducous that shoots at the apical
sectors are often denuded, leaving only the lobules and it is difficult to find a shoot with intact leaves,
such features were also drawn by Castle (1963: fig. 14a) (shown here in Fig. 8d). Radula caespitosa
hardly resembles any of the characters of R. madagascariensis, and here it is treated as a distinct
species.

    Schistochila macrodonta is known almost only from the type and no recent collection
94                                    1. Hattori Bot. Lab. No. 93                             2 0 0 3

from China is available. The two other collections, one as recent as 2000, were from
Bhutan. This species seems to be a giant form of S. aligera ; the leaf apex is broadly obtuse,
the leaf margins are almost entire, with only angulations and the cell size is similar. Some
of the shoots resemble S. aligera in having oblong, linear ventral leaf lobes with small
teeth. However, unlike S. aligera, the base of the dorsal leaf lobe of S. macrodonta is very
ampliate. Until more specimens are available, S. macrodonta is here provisionally main-
tained as a distinct species.
      In the original description, bicellular propagules at the apices of leaves were men-
tioned. An examination of the type material as well as the recent collection from Bhutan
reveals that among the several shoots present, only one of them has masses of smaller cells
fringing the broken margins of a few leaves at the apex. These cells resemble the gemmae
so often seen in members of Radula species. Members of Schistochila are not known to
produce gemmae and their presence in S. macrodonta is rather unusual (Fig. 7: d).

     Schistochila nuda differs from all species of Schistochila in this region by its very
small size and entire leaf margins. The shoots are soft-textured and both dorsal and ventral
leaf lobes have very rounded apices, features enough to separate it from all other species of
Schistochila in Asia. Apparently it has a restricted distribution.

                            The structure of a typical leafy liverwort.

    The leaves of many species are divided into lobes, which may be entire, rounded,
pointed or ciliate. The number of lobes, their size and their shape can all be important
diagnostic features. Leaves with two lobes are said to be bifid or 2-fid; those with
three lobes trifid or 3-fid, with four lobes 4-fid, etc. In some genera the lobes themselves
are also subdivided. Leaf margins can be entire, dentate, ciliate or toothed. In all
leafy liverworts the leaf margins lack a border typical of many mosses.
    The leaves of some genera, such as Frullania and Radula, have lobules, which
are merely folds in the leaf near the point of attachment to the stem. The presence of
lobules, and their size and shape can aid identification. There can be a lobule on the
dorsal or ventral side of the leaf, but never both, and lobules can be easily overlooked
under a hand lens.
                                                                           Introduction


    The sporophytes of leafy liverworts comprise a uniform, globular or near-globular
capsule atop a translucent white seta and are rarely useful for identifying species.
Unlike moss capsules, those of liverworts mature before the seta grows. There is no
calyptra or operculum, and the capsules split along weaknesses in the capsule wall to
release the spores.
    As in mosses, most leafy liverworts are anchored to their substrata by rhizoids,
but tubers are never present. The stems of leafy liverworts are also much the same as
those of mosses, but they never have paraphyllia or pseudoparaphyllia.



The etructure of hornworte
At first glance a hornwort might be mistaken for a thallose liverwort, but there are
easily distinguishable differences. The gametophyte consists of a thallus that is usually
dark green and fleshy, and attached to moist soil with many smooth rhizoids. The
mcist obvious difference is in the sporophyte, which consists of a long, needle-like
capsule arising from a short, cylindrical involucre.·En masse the sporophytes have
something of the look of grass seedlings. At maturity the capsule splits along its
whole length, beginning at the top. Under the microscope the cells can be seen to
contain (in general) only one or two large chloroplasts; in contrast, the cells of most
liverworts contain numerous small chloroplasts. The antheridia and archegonia are
contained in separate cavities sunk into the thallus surface. In some species there are
also cavities in the upper surface in which minute colonies of Nostoc cyanobacteria

      Radula buccinifera Radulaceae                                                 WTVAN
     The genus Radula is characterised by more or less oval, incubous
     leaves with a simple lobule and no underleaves. This is by far the
     commonest species in the region. It is epiphytic on trees, tree-ferns
     and shrubs, and can also be found on stones and rocks. In its perfect
     form it grows in a series of overlapping mats standing out from the
     substratum. The leaves are rather flat and the lobule is small and
     diamo�d-shaped. There is a distinct notch where the leaf lobe and
     lobule join. The capsule develops within a tubular but flattened
     perianth - the general form of perianths in most other Radula species.
          R. acutiloba (VN) and R. papulosa (N) both commonly carry

Cheilolejeunea mimosa Lejeuneaceae                                            STVAN
There is litt}e to separate this species from its cousins in the genus
Lejeunea, the tax.anomic differences being microscopic and
impossible to detect in the field. There may also be other
Cheilolejeunea species in the region. However, it is possible to at
least tentatively identify this species from its size and general
appearance. It grows on the bark of trees and shrubs in damp to
wet forest. The shoots are pale olive, mostly less than 1 mm wide,
so that the plant looks rather like a minute Frullania or Radula, with which it is
commonly mixed. It is not uncommon to find it growing on those liverworts. The
incubou�, densely overlapping leaves are rounded or sometimes slightly pointed. The
ventral lobule is a simple, narrowly triangular fold at the point of attachment to the
steni, forming a notch in the leaf margin. The lobule shape is the major distinction
from other genera. The underleave� are almost circular and bilobed, often to half-
way.

 Frullania clavata Frullaniaceae      -                                           TVAN
Frullania species are recognisable in the fieJd by a
combination of habit, habitat, colour, the presence
of rounded, inflated lobules, and the presence of
underleaves. The shoots cling tightly to bark or
rocks, although the upper layers of large plants can
be rather loose. They are most common in dry to
damp forest and woodland, but some species are
found in wet forest and rainforest.                      f clavata          f scandens
     F. clavata is perhaps the largest of the species
in the region, with shoots 2 mm or more wide. It
seems to be confined mainly to m:oist environments.
H is always epiphytic and is usually green with a
tinge of brown or chestnut. The lobules are claw­
�haped, curving downward to a point. The circular
underleaves are notched at the apex, and the tips

or-less transversely on the narrow stems, and are angled towards
 the shoot apex. Each leaf is deeply divided into 4 triangular
 lobes. The underleaves are smaller and also 4-fid.

   Lejeunea drummondii Lejeuneaceae                                             WSTVAN
   The habitat and leaf shape of this species are not sufficient to
  distinguish it from Frullania species, so that an examination of the
  _ventral lobules is needed. The overlapping shoots are 2-3 mm wide,
   forming patches on the bark of trees or shrubs in damp to wet habitats.
  Tibey are a clear green when moist, but dry to a drab olive-green.
  'I'he densely overlapping leaves are arranged alternately and
  incubously on the stem, which is invisible on the dorsal side. They
  are shortly oval in outline but are often slightly pointed, especially those nearer the
  shoot tips. The lobules are inflated and ratker diamond-shaped, with a small apical
• tooth that is sometimes not easily seen. Underleaves are-roughly circular, bifid to
: abouf half way.
       The size and lobule shape distinguish this species from Cheilolejeunea mimosa,
  and the presence of underleaves separate it from Radula species. There are numerous
  potentially confusing Lejeuneaceae but they are all very much smaller, except
  Acrolejeunea secwifolia (VN), which has unlobed underleaves.

Similar species: Cheilolejeunea mimos_a, other Lejeuneaceae spp., Frullania spp.,
Radula spp.

 Mosses & Allied Plants of Southern Australia


Lepidozia glaucophylla Lepidoziaceae                                              TVN
The glaucous bluish colour is enough to distinguish this from
most other species. The prostrate, sparsely pinnate shoots creep
over soil, usually in loose patches, in sheltered crevices and
overhangs. Erect, pinnate fronds can develop in very moist,
densely shaded habitats. The glaucous leaves are rather
asymmetrical and deeply 4-fid, widely spaced on the main
stems but overlapping on the ends of branches. We have never
seen capsules. Telaranea centipes, which can look similar at       leaf and underleaf

Mosses and their close
relatives, the liverworts and
hornworts, are found in almost every
corner of every continent, from the wettest
forest to the driest desert. This field guide brings
the identification of these small plants within easy reach
of everyone, from home gardeners to students, naturalists
and professional botanists. Written in a fresh, easy-to-read
style and illustrated with startlingly beautiful photographs

Lejeuneaceae




                                                                                 Downloaded from http://connectsci.au/books/monograph-pdf/6171/9780643101210.pdf by U
Species of the Lejeuneaceae have leaves in two or thrce rows, with those
in the lateral rows having their margins folded to Iorm a very small lobe
(lobule) on the underside. The family contains some of the smallest
bryophytes found in Tasmanian rainforest.

88. Co1ol~eun.mlaevigata is one of only a few obligately epiphyllous
    liverworts in Tasmania. The fern Blechnum wattsii is a cornmon host
    species.

89. Colura saccophylla is a tiny epiphyte, only just visible to the naked eye.
    It closely resembles C. pulchemima var. bartlettii and xnicroscopic
    characters are needed to separate the two.

90. Uiplasiolejeur~eaplicatiloba is a minute epiphyte on twigs and is found
    mostly in the canopy. The leaves are widely spaced on the stem and
    their shape resembles an old-fashioned bonnet.

91. Drepanolqeunea aucklar~dicais a common trunk epiphyte but is easily
    overlooked because of its very small s i ~ eand tendency to straggle
    amongst other bryophytes. The leaves are lance-shaped, with an
    acute tip.

    viewed from the upper surface, but the presence of underlcaves will
    separate it from Radula and the shape of the lobules from Fmllunia.

Radulaceae




                                                                            Downloaded from http://connectsci.au/books/monograph-pdf/6171/9780643101210.pdf by U
Members of this family often resemble species of Frul1an.i~when viewed
from the upper surface but they are easily separated by the absence of
underleaves and the different shape of the leaf lobule. The bracts
(perianth) which surround the young sporophytes are flattened, giving
them a distinctive appearance.

110. Radula bucci.n@a is epiphytic or occurs on rocks. The leaves are no
     as closely overlapping as those in many other species of the gcnus
     and sonieti~nesthey scarcely touch. The lobules are small and
     diamond shaped.

11 1. Radula cornpacta often has a circular growth lorm and is one of [he
      most common and widespread species of Raduln in Tasmania. The
      leaf lobules are comparatively large and almost rectangular in
      shape.

112. Radula multiczrnentula is a golderi-brown epiphyte characterised by
     miniature leafy shoots on the undersurl'ace next to every leaf.

WIKIPEDIA  (1) 
The radula is an anatomical structure found in most mollusks, serving as their primary feeding tool. Often compared to a tongue, this minutely toothed, chitinous ribbon typically functions by scraping or cutting food before it enters the esophagus. Mollusks in every class possess a radula, except for bivalves, which instead employ waving cilia to draw in minute organisms for feeding.
